On the Mixtures and Powers of Simple Drugs

De simplicium medicamentorum temperamentis ac facultatibus · Kühn XI 379-892 and XII 1-377 · books 1-11

No complete published translation into English by a scholar that we know of. An English translation of books 1-5 by J. Wilkins is announced in the Cambridge Galen Translations. Books 6-8: a French translation with the Greek by C. Manco (doctoral thesis, Montpellier and Bologna, 2020). The prefaces of books 6-11 and the first two entries of book 6: English by M. Coughlin (ancientmedicine.org, 2023). All eleven books: a free French version by H. Fayard (1548). An unreviewed machine translation into English of Kühn XI and XII, the volumes containing these books, was posted at Source Library (sourcelibrary.org) during 2026. This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. The small grey numbers are the pages of Kühn's Greek edition in books 1-5 and the entry numbers (book.chapter.entry) in books 6-11. Passages with a red rule failed the machine checks; those checks are automated agreement tests, not a reading of the Greek, so a passage without the rule is not checked either. Words in square brackets were supplied by the model for the sense; Greek in parentheses gives the original word; any remark about a doubtful or corrupt reading is the model's, not a scholar's.

Check (a): the English back-translated into Greek by a third model shares at least half of the unit's term lemmas with the source. Check (b): the two renderings agree on at least 70% of the unit's target terms. Check (c): a model judge, salted with 10 planted errors and 10 anchors it had to get right, finds at least 80% of the term renderings within the LSJ entry's senses, with at most 2 outside. A unit fails when it fails two of three.

Flags, under each passage: places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors. Sentence by sentence, each rendering is compared with the Greek for numbers, negations and words of more or less, and for the names of drugs and parts of the body (a name in the English with none in the Greek, or a name in the Greek with none in the English). These are word-matching tests: a flag often comes from a sentence pair that is aligned a little off, a paraphrase or a name the model kept in Greek, and a passage with no flag is not checked by them either. The flags play no part in the unit verdicts.

Greek (Kühn)
εἰ δέ τις ἄχθεται τῷ μήκει τῶν λόγων, οὐχ ἡμῖν χρὴ μέμφεσθαι τοῦτον, ἀλλὰ τοῖς τὰ 541 πιθανὰ μὲν, οὐκ ἀληθῆ δὲ γράψασιν ἰατροῖς. ὡς ἔγωγε καὶ νῦν ἔτι δέδοικα μὴ διὰ τὸ τάχος τῆς πρὸς αὐτοὺς ἀντιλογίας ἔνια τῶν σοφισμάτων παρακρούσηται τοὺς ὁμιλοῦντας αὐτοῖς νέους. εἰ μὲν γὰρ γεγυμνασμένοι κατὰ τὰς λογικὰς μεθόδους καὶ γιγνώσκοντες ὡς χρὴ λύειν σοφίσματα προσήρχοντο ταῖς τῶν βιβλίων ἀναγνώσεσιν, οὐκ ἂν ἔλαθον αὐτοὺς οὔθ’ οἱ παραλογιζόμενοί τι κατὰ τὰς ἀποδείξεις οὔθ’ ἡμεῖς συντόμως αὐτὸ τἀληθὲς ἐκδιδάσκοντες. ἐπεὶ δ’ ἀγύμναστοί τέ εἰσιν οἱ πλείους τῶν ἰατρῶν αὐτῶν ἐν τοῖσδε καί τινες πρὸς τούτῳ καὶ ἀργότεροι φύσει τὸν λογισμὸν, εἰ μὴ τῆς παρ’ ἡμῶν ἐπιτύχοιεν οὗτοι βοη θείας ἐλεγχόντων τὰ σοφίσματα, πεισθήσονται ῥᾳδίως ὑπὸ τῶν σοφιστῶν, ὥστε ὅσῳ μὲν ἐπὶ τοῖσδε κᾂν λείπειν τι δόξαι τῇ πραγματείᾳ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὅσῳ μὲν ἐπὶ τοῖσδε κᾂν λείπειν τι δόξαι
But if anyone is vexed by the length of the discussions, he ought not to blame us for this, but rather the physicians who have written things 541 that are plausible but not true. For I myself even now still fear that, because of the brevity of the refutation directed against them, some of the sophisms may lead astray the young men who associate with them. For if they came to the reading of the books trained in the logical methods and knowing how one ought to resolve sophisms, neither would those who commit some fallacy in their demonstrations escape their notice, nor would we, when we teach the truth itself concisely. But since the majority of the physicians themselves are unexercised in these matters, and some, in addition to this, are also by nature rather sluggish in their reasoning, if these men should not obtain the assistance from us of refuting the sophisms, they will easily be persuaded by the sophists; so that, as far as these men are concerned, the treatise might even seem to be lacking in something.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τάχος, ὅσῳ μὲν ἐπὶ τοῖσδε, κᾂν λείπειν τι δόξαι
If anyone is annoyed at the length of the discussions, he ought not to blame us, but the physicians who wrote things 541 that are plausible but not true. For I, for my part, am even now still afraid that, because of the haste of my refutation of them, some of the sophisms may lead astray the young men who keep company with them. For if they came to the reading of the books trained in the logical methods and knowing how sophisms must be resolved, neither those who commit some fallacy in their demonstrations would escape their notice, nor would we, who concisely teach the truth itself. But since most of the physicians themselves are untrained in these matters, and some, in addition to this, are also by nature rather sluggish in reasoning, these men, unless they obtain help from us refuting the sophisms, will easily be persuaded by the sophists; so that, as far as these men are concerned, the treatise might even seem to be lacking something.

K11.541-542K. XI 541–542, ch. 3.1, 3.2 difficulty low (1.16/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ἀλλὰ γὰρ ἐπεὶ μὴ δυνατόν ἐστιν ἁπάντων στοχάζεσθαι τῶν ἀνεγνωσμένων ἀνομοίων ὑπαρχόντων, οὐ ταῖς φύσεσι μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ταῖς ἡλικίαις καὶ ταῖς ἕξεσιν, ὃ μάλιστα τοῖς πλείστοις ἐδόκει χρήσιμον ἔσεσθαι 542 λόγων εἶδος ἐν τοῖς πρὸ τούτου δυοῖν ὑπομνήμασιν ἐποιησάμην, ἐν μὲν τῷ πρώτῳ περὶ ὄξους καὶ ὕδατος δυνάμεως ἐπισκεψάμενος, ἐν δὲ τῷ δευτέρῳ τὰ πλεῖστα μὲν ὑπὲρ ἐλαίου διῆλθον, ὀλίγα δέ τινα καὶ περὶ ῥοδίνου προσθείς. ἐν δὲ τῷ τρίτῳ τῷδε ποιήσομεν τοιοῦτον τὸν λόγον ὁποῖον εὐθὺς ἐξ ἀρχῆς ἐγνώκειν μεταχειρίσασθαι, διελόμενος μὲν τὰ σημαινόμενα πρῶτον ἑκάστου τῶν ὀνομάτων· ἐφεξῆς δὲ τὰ οἷον στοιχεῖα τῆς πραγματείας ἁπάσης διελθὼν, εἶθ’ οὕτως ἐπὶ τὰς οἷον συλλαβὰς ἀφικόμενος, ἐξ ὧν ἤδη τὸ τέλος αὐτὸ τῆς πραγματείας, ἐφ’ ὃ σπεύδομεν, ἑτοίμως ἕψεται. Ἡ μὲν δὴ τῶν ὀνομάτων διαίρεσις οὐδὲν περιττότερον ἐκδιδάσκει τῆς ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ περὶ κράσεως εἰρημένης κᾀν τῷ πρώτῳ τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων, ἀναμνήσω δ’ αὐτῆς καὶ νῦν. ἕκαστον τῶν σωμάτων ἤτοι θερμὸν, ἢ ψυχρὸν, ἢ ξηρὸν, ἢ ὑγρὸν λέγεταί τε καὶ φαίνεται ποτὲ μὲν κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν ἐστὶν, ποτὲ δὲ ἐπίκτητόν τινα ποιότητα προσειληφός.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀνεγνωσμένων, ἐστὶν
But since it is not possible to aim at all the readers, who are dissimilar not only in their natures but also in their ages and their conditions, the kind of discourse which seemed likely to be most useful to the greatest number I adopted in the two commentaries before this one 542: in the first I examined the power of vinegar and of water, while in the second I went through for the most part the subject of oil, adding also a few remarks about rose oil. In this third one, however, we shall make the discourse of the kind which I had resolved right from the beginning to undertake, first distinguishing the meanings of each of the names; next going through the elements, as it were, of the whole treatise, and then in this way arriving at the syllables, as it were, from which the very end of the treatise, towards which we are hastening, will now readily follow. Now the division of the names teaches nothing further beyond what was said in the third book On Mixture and in the first of these commentaries, but I shall recall it now as well. Each of the bodies is called, and appears, either hot or cold or dry or wet, sometimes being so in accordance with its own nature, sometimes having taken on some acquired quality.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀνεγνωσμένων (perfect passive, 'things read'; the sense requires 'readers', so ἀνεγνωκότων may be the intended reading), ἐστὶν (in 'ποτὲ μὲν κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν ἐστὶν' the syntax is awkward and the word may be corrupt, perhaps for ὄν)
But since it is not possible to aim at all those who read [these books], since they are unlike not only in their natures but also in their ages and conditions, in the two books before this one I adopted that form 542 of discourse which seemed likely to be most useful to the majority: in the first I examined the power of vinegar and water, and in the second I went through mostly matters concerning oil, adding a few things about rose oil as well. In this third book I shall give the discourse the form I had resolved from the very beginning to undertake. I shall first distinguish the meanings of each of the names; next I shall go through what are, as it were, the elements of the whole treatise; and then I shall come in this way to what are, as it were, the syllables, from which the very end of the treatise, towards which we are hastening, will readily follow. Now the division of the names teaches nothing further than what was said in the third book On Mixtures and in the first of these books, but I shall remind you of it now as well. Each body is both said and seen to be either hot, or cold, or dry, or wet: sometimes it is so according to its own nature, sometimes because it has taken on some acquired quality.

K11.542-543K. XI 542–543, ch. 3.2 difficulty low (1.31/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 14/14 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ἔθος δ’ ἐστὶν ἡμῖν λέγειν τὸ μὲν κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν πρώτως τε καὶ καθ’ ἑαυτὸ καὶ δ’ ἑαυτὸ 543 δυνάμει τοιοῦτον ὑπάρχειν τε καὶ φαίνεσθαι· τὸ δὲ τὴν ἐπίκτητον προσειληφὸς ποιότητα κατά τι συμβεβηκὸς εἶναι καὶ φαίνεσθαι τοιοῦτον. αὐτῶν δὲ τῶν κατὰ τὴν οἰκείαν φύσιν ἤτοι θερμῶν, ἢ ψυχρῶν, ἢ ξηρῶν, ἢ ὑγρῶν εἶναι λεγομένων, τὰ μὲν ἐνεργείᾳ τοιαῦτα ὑπάρχει, τὰ δὲ δυνάμει, διαφέροντος τοῦδε τοῦ νῦν εἰρημένου δυνάμει παρὰ τὸ μικρῷ πρόσθεν ῥηθέν· ἐκεῖνο μὲν γὰρ τῷ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ἀντιδιῄρητο, τουτὶ δὲ τῷ κατ’ ἐνέργειαν. αὐτῶν δὲ τῶν κατ’ ἐνέργειαν τὸ μὲν ἁπλῶς λέγεται θερμὸν, ἢ ψυχρὸν, ἢ ὑγρὸν, ἢ ξηρὸν, ὥσπερ τὰ στοιχεῖα, τὸ δὲ κατ’ ἐπικράτειαν, ὥσπερ ἄνθρωπος καὶ ἵππος καὶ βοῦς καὶ κύων καί ὅλα τὰ ζῶα· τὸ δέ τι πρὸς τὸ σύμμετρον ὁμογενὲς, ἢ ὁμοειδὲς, ὥσπερ εἰ τύχοι λέων μὲν ἀνθρώπῳ παραβαλλόμενος, ἄνθρωπος δ’ ἀκμάζων παιδί. τὸ δὲ πρὸς ὁτιοῦν τὸ ἐπιτυχὸν, οἷον ὁ Δίων πρὸς τὸν Θέωνα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δυνάμει (first occurrence, in the phrase καθ’ ἑαυτὸ καὶ δι’ ἑαυτὸ δυνάμει), ὅλα τὰ ζῶα
It is our custom to say that what is such according to its own nature is, and appears, such primarily and in itself and through itself, 543 potentially (δυνάμει); whereas what has taken on an acquired quality is, and appears, such according to some accident. And of those things themselves which are said to be hot, or cold, or dry, or wet according to their proper nature, some are such in actuality, others potentially — this 'potentially' now spoken of differing from the one said a little before; for that one was set in contrast with 'accidentally', whereas this one with 'in actuality'. And of those which are such in actuality, one is called hot, or cold, or wet, or dry simply, like the elements; another by predominance, like a man and a horse and an ox and a dog and all the animals as wholes; another in some respect relative to the well-proportioned thing of the same genus or of the same species, as, for instance, a lion compared with a man, or a man in his prime with a child; and another relative to anything whatever one happens upon, as Dion relative to Theon.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δ’ ἑαυτὸ (taken as δι’ ἑαυτό, 'through itself'), δυνάμει (in the first sentence; its construction there is awkward, but it is taken as 'in potentiality', in line with the later back-reference), ὅλα τὰ ζῶα (rendered 'animals generally')
It is our custom to say that what is such according to its own nature is, and appears, such primarily, per se and through itself, 543 in potentiality; but that what has taken on the acquired quality is, and appears, such by some accident. And of those things themselves which are said to be hot, or cold, or dry, or moist according to their proper nature, some are such in actuality, others in potentiality. This 'in potentiality' now spoken of differs from the one mentioned a little before: for that one was distinguished as the opposite of 'by accident', whereas this one is distinguished as the opposite of 'in actuality'. And of those things themselves which are such in actuality, one is called hot, or cold, or moist, or dry simply, as are the elements; another by predominance, as a man, a horse, an ox, a dog, and animals generally; another in relation to the well-proportioned of the same genus or of the same species, as, for instance, a lion compared with a man, and a man in his prime compared with a child; and another in relation to any chance thing whatever, as Dion in relation to Theon.

K11.543-544K. XI 543–544, ch. 3.2, 3.3 difficulty low (1.08/100; thin 1, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 15/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
καὶ δὴ καὶ τῶν δυνάμει λεγομένων σωμάτων εἶναι τοίων ἢ τοίων ἀναγκαῖον ἔφαμεν ἕκαστον ὡς πρὸς τὸ κατ’ ἐνέργειαν ἀναφερόμενον λέγεσθαι, τὸ μὲν ὅτι ῥᾳδίως ἐκπυροῦται πρὸς τὸ κατ’ 544 ἐνέργειαν ἁπλῶς θερμὸν, τὸ δ’ ὅτι τὰ ζῶα θερμαίνει πρὸς τὸ κατ’ ἐπικράτειαν. οὕτω δὲ καὶ τὸ μὲν ἄνθρωπον θερμαῖνον ὡς πρὸς ἄνθρωπον εἶναι δυνάμει θερμὸν, τὸ δὲ βοῦν ἢ ἵππον ὡς πρὸς βοῦν ἢ ἵππον. ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ τὸ μὲν τῷδέ τινι τῷ ἀνθρώπῳ, τὸ δὲ θερμὸν ἢ ψυχρὸν ἢ ὑγρὸν ὑπάρχον ἐκείνου μόνον τῇ φύσει παραβαλλόμενον. And indeed, of the bodies that are said to be potentially of such or such a kind, we said that each is necessarily so called with reference to what is such in actuality: the one, because it is easily set ablaze, with reference to what is simply hot in 544 actuality; the other, because it heats animals, with reference to what is hot by predominance. So too, what heats a human being is potentially hot in relation to a human being, and what heats an ox or a horse, in relation to an ox or a horse. And similarly, one thing is so in relation to this particular human being, another being hot or cold or moist when compared only with the nature of that one. From this distinction of the names we derived benefit for the recognition of their mixture: if something were said to be hot, or cold, or dry, or moist in relation to a human being, we deemed it right to leave aside all the other animals and to make the trial and the test on the human being alone; but if in relation to an ox, or a horse, or a deer, or any one of the other animals, that the trial should again in turn be made on that one alone. And in these matters we also censured those who try to discover the powers of the drugs from the consistency, or colour, or heaviness, or lightness, or the quality presented to taste or to smell by each of them.

Moreover, of the bodies said to be potentially of this or that kind, we stated that each must necessarily be spoken of as referred to what is so in actuality: the one, because it is readily set on fire, in relation to what is simply hot in 544 actuality; the other, because it heats animals, in relation to what is so by predominance. In these discussions we also censured those who attempt to discover the powers of drugs from the consistency, or colour, or weight, or lightness, or the quality of each of the drugs with respect to taste or smell.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εἰδοποιῶν
All these things, then, appear to have followed upon the correct division of the names; 545 and the elements, as it were, of the inquiry before us we showed to lie in the knowledge of the primary qualities, which we demonstrated in the work On the Elements according to Hippocrates—moistness and dryness, hotness and coldness; and the discovery of these too came to us, one may almost say, from correctly distinguishing the things signified by the names. For by marking off a drug from nourishment in that the former alters in respect of quality, whereas the latter is assimilated to the substance of the body, we were guided from there, in the course of the argument, to the discovery of the qualities that alter us. And since alterations are twofold in genus—some being species-producing, which indeed are properly and primarily named alterations, others breaking apart and drawing together the parts of our body, which people call alterations by a misuse of the term rather than by proper naming—we say that the powers of drugs are contained in the first of these.

All these things, then, evidently followed from the correct division of the names. 545 And the elements, as it were, of the present inquiry we showed to lie in the knowledge of the primary qualities, which we demonstrated in the work On the Elements according to Hippocrates: moistness and dryness, heat and coldness. Indeed, our discovery of these too came about almost entirely from correctly distinguishing the meanings of the names. For having distinguished drug from food by this, that the one alters according to quality while the other is assimilated to the substance of the body, we were guided from there by reasoning to the discovery of the qualities that alter us. Alterations are twofold in genus. Some are form-producing, and these are properly and primarily named alterations. Others break up and draw together the parts of our body, and people call these alterations by a misuse of the term rather than by naming them properly. Since this is so, we say that the powers of drugs are contained in the first kind.

For nothing, [we say,] is able to change from one species into another species without being heated and cooled and dried and 546 moistened. And I mean "from species into species" when, out of bread and ptisane and lentil-soup, blood and phlegm and bile, both yellow and black, come to be, and out of these in turn bone and soft fat and sinew and flesh, artery and vein, and each of the other parts of the animal. But if something, by cutting us, as glass or a sword does, or by bruising, as stone and lead do, or by drawing together the parts that have been separated, as bandaging does, alters these parts in some way, [we say] that such things are not drugs; for an alteration of this kind does not remove each of the things that exist from its own proper nature. For whether you break bread into many pieces and cut it up, or whether you gather it together into one out of many small pieces, it does not depart from its proper form, nor does bread become flesh when it is broken down, nor bone if it should happen to be brought together in this way; but when, in the course of being concocted in the belly and the veins, it changes into blood and phlegm, and then out of these into bone and flesh and the other parts of the body, it is altered in its whole substance and departs from its original nature, passing over into another species.

For nothing, we say, can change from one form to another without being heated and cooled and dried and 546 moistened. I mean 'from form to form' when from bread and barley-gruel and lentils there come to be blood and phlegm and bile, both yellow and black, and from these in turn bone and fat and nerve and flesh, artery and vein, and each of the other parts of the animal. Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Yet all the affections of the body were shown to receive their alterations and changes of substance into another form from nothing other than the hot, the cold, the dry 547 and the moist. And accordingly we said that the primary and elemental form-producing qualities are four—moistness, dryness, heat, coldness—and we inquire, for each of the drugs, which of these it possesses as predominant in its mixture. Straightaway, then, on this first point most of those who have investigated them seem to me to have gone wrong, in that they examine the predominant quality simply, as certain natural philosophers do; and indeed many of them, reading the writings of Theophrastus and Aristotle unmethodically, reason fallaciously. And yet the difference is very great between making distinctions concerning hot and cold and dry and moist with a view to the whole of nature, and doing so with a view to human beings only. For when one looks to the whole of nature, each of these is said in two ways: in one sense simply, in the other by predominance.

each of the elements simply, but a human being and 548 a horse and an ox by predominance. But when the examination is carried out in relation to something, one must consider only that in relation to which it is said. And what we are inquiring is not whether the moist is simply greater than the dry in sea-water—for this we see plainly—but whether it is so in relation to a human being. Nor indeed even in relation to him when compared simply in this way, as if you were to compare a lion with him as well; for even so sea-water is moister than a human being. How, then, do we still inquire whether sea-water is dry in its power? How else than in the sense equivalent to its being drying of the human body? To this alone, then, must one look, leaving the rest aside. But most physicians do the opposite, for they look at everything else rather than at this. How, then, having turned toward the human being and withdrawn from the rest, shall we come to know how sea-water stands in respect of activity toward him?

Greek (Kühn)
ἢ δηλονότι προσφέροντες τῷ σώματι καὶ πεῖραν τοῦ γιγνομένου λαμβάνοντες ἔν τε τῷ λούειν ἐν θαλάττῃ καὶ καταβρέχειν ἢ καταντλεῖν, ἐπιτιθέναι τε πολλάκις μορίοις ἔριά τέ καὶ σπόγγους θαλάττῃ δεύσαντας, ἐπιπλάσμασί τε μιγνύναι καὶ κηρωταῖς, 549 κᾀν τούτοις ἅπασιν διορίσασθαι τοῦ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς ἀποβαίνοντός ποτε τὸ κατὰ τὴν οἰκείαν αὐτοῦ τοῦ φαρμάκου δύναμιν ἀποτελούμενον; ἐν τούτῳ γάρ ἐστι τὸ πᾶν κῦρος τῆς εὑρέσεως, οὗ μόνον σχεδὸν οὐδεὶς αὐτῶν ἅπτεται. ἀλλ’ αἱ τῶν φιλοσόφων ἀποφάσεις ἐξαπατῶσιν αὐτοὺς, οὐ δυναμένους αἰσθάνεσθαι τῆς ὁμωνυμίας. Οὐ μόνον γὰρ, ὡς ἀρτίως εἴρηται, τὸ μὲν ἁπλῶς, τὸ δ’ ἐπικρατείᾳ λέγεται πρὸς αὐτῶν. ἁπλῶς μὲν γὰρ τὸ πῦρ θερμὸν, ἐπικρατείᾳ δ’ ἄνθρωπος, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὸ κατ’ ἐπικράτειαν αὐτὸ διχῶς λέγεσθαι νομίζουσιν, τὸ μὲν κατὰ τοὺς ὄγκους τῆς οὐσίας, ὡς κᾀν τοῖς περὶ κράσεων ὑπομνήμασιν ἡμεῖς διῃροῦμεν, οἷον εἰ δύο χοὰς ὕδατος ἄκρως ζέοντος ἀναμίξαις ἑνὶ χοῒ ψυχροῦ παντάπασιν ὕδατος, ἕτερον δὲ τὸ κατὰ τὴν τῶν ποιοτήτων ἐνέργειαν. τοῦτο δ’ ἤδη δόγματος ἐχόμενον λέγουσιν, οὐκ ἔτ’ ἐκ τῶν ἐναργῶς φαινομένων λαμβάνοντες.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κῦρος, καταντλεῖν
Clearly, by applying it to the body and taking trial of what happens, both in bathing in sea-water and in drenching or fomenting with it, and in frequently placing upon parts wool and sponges soaked in sea-water, and in mixing it with plasters and cerates, 549 and in all these cases distinguishing from what sometimes results per accidens that which is accomplished in accordance with the proper power of the drug itself? For in this lies the whole decisive force of the discovery, and this alone almost none of them touches upon. But the assertions of the philosophers deceive them, since they are unable to perceive the homonymy. For not only, as was said just now, is one thing said by them to be so simply and another by predominance — for fire is hot simply, but a human being by predominance — but they also hold that 'by predominance' itself is said in two ways: the one according to the bulks of the substance, as we too distinguished in the treatises On Mixtures, as for instance if you were to mix two choes of water boiling to the utmost degree with one chous of altogether cold water; the other according to the activity of the qualities. But this latter they state as something already dependent upon a doctrine, no longer taking it from what evidently appears.

Greek (Kühn)
ὑποθέμενοι γὰρ τὸ μὲν ψυχρὸν συνάγειν καὶ πιλεῖν καὶ ἀκίνητον ἐργάζεσθαι τὴν οὐσίαν, τὸ δὲ θερμὸν ἀραιοῦν καὶ διαχεῖν καὶ πέττειν καὶ ﻿ 550 κινεῖν εὐλόγως ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς φυτοῖς κρατεῖν ἀποφαίνονται τὸ θερμόν. μηδὲ γὰρ ἂν μήτε τὴν ἐκ τῆς γῆς ἀνάδοσιν τῆς τροφῆς ἐπὶ μήκιστον ἄλλως γενέσθαι, μήτ’ ἀλλοίωσιν αὐτοῦ μήτε πέψιν, ἀλλὰ μηδὲ τὴν αὔξησιν ὅλως τῶν φυτῶν ἢ τὴν βλάστησιν, ἢ τῶν καρπῶν τὴν γένεσιν, εἰ μὴ τὸ θερμὸν ἐκράτει· καὶ οὕτως ἤδη πάλιν ἐν αὐτοῖς τοῖς φυτοῖς τὸ μὲν θερμότερον ἀποφαίνουσιν, τὸ δὲ ψυχρότερον. ἐκ ταύτης οὖν τῆς ὑποθέσεως ἤρτηται καὶ ὁ περὶ τῶν ῥόδων αὐτῶν λόγος ὅτι θερμά. κρατεῖ γὰρ ἐπὶ πλέον ἐν αὐτοῖς τὸ θερμὸν ἢ ἐν ἄλλοις τισὶ φυτοῖς. ἐξ ἑτέρας δ’ αὖ πάλιν ὑποθέσεως ὁ περὶ ἐλαίου τε καὶ πιμελῆς. ὅτι γὰρ ἂν ἐκφλογοῦται τάχιστα, διὰ τοῦτο θερμὰ τὴν δύναμιν εἶναί φασιν αὐτά. καὶ καθ’ ἕτερον δ’ αὖ τρόπον ὅσα γλυκέα, διότι γὰρ οἰκειότατα καὶ ὁμοιότατα ταῖς κράσεσιν ἡμῶν φύσει θερμῶν ὑπαρχόντων, διὰ τοῦτο καὶ ταῦτα θερμὰ εἶναί φασι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπὶ μήκιστον, αὐτοῦ, ὅτι γὰρ ἂν ἐκφλογοῦται
For having assumed that the cold draws together and condenses the substance and renders it immobile, whereas the hot rarefies and diffuses and concocts and 550 moves it, they reasonably declare that the hot prevails in all plants. For neither, they say, would the distribution of nutriment from the earth to the greatest extent otherwise come about, nor its alteration, nor its concoction, nor indeed the growth of plants at all, or their sprouting, or the generation of fruits, if the hot did not prevail; and so then again among the plants themselves they declare one to be hotter, another colder. On this assumption, then, depends also their account concerning roses, that they are hot: for the hot prevails to a greater degree in them than in certain other plants. And from another assumption again, in turn, the account concerning oil and soft fat: for because they burst into flame very quickly, on this account they say that they are hot in power. And in yet another way again, all things that are sweet: for because they are most akin and most similar to our mixtures, we being hot by nature, on this account they say that these too are hot.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπὶ μήκιστον, ὅτι γὰρ ἂν ἐκφλογοῦται, ἀλλοίωσιν αὐτοῦ
For, having laid down as a hypothesis that the cold draws together and compresses and renders the substance motionless, while the hot rarefies and diffuses and concocts and 550 moves, they reasonably declare that in all plants the hot prevails. For neither, they say, would the distribution of the nourishment from the earth to a very great distance come about otherwise, nor its alteration nor its concoction, nor indeed the growth of plants at all, or their sprouting, or the generation of their fruits, unless the hot prevailed; and so in this way they go on to declare, among the plants themselves in turn, one hotter and another colder. On this hypothesis, then, depends also the argument about roses themselves, that they are hot: for the hot prevails in them to a greater extent than in certain other plants. And from yet another hypothesis, again, comes the argument about oil and fat: for because they blaze up very quickly, for this reason they say that they are hot in power. And in yet another way, again, whatever things are sweet: for because they are most akin and most similar to our mixtures, which are by nature hot, for this reason they say that these too are hot.

K11.550-551K. XI 550–551, ch. 3.6 difficulty mid (1.7/100; thin 1, absent 2) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
τοῖς μὲν οὖν φιλοσόφοις, ὡς ἂν περὶ τῆς ὅλης φύσεως ἀποφαινομένοις, ὀρθῶς τὰ τοιαῦτα ζητεῖται καὶ λέγεται· τοῖς δ’ ἰατροῖς, ἕτερον ἔχουσι 551 σκοπὸν, οὐκ ὀρθῶς· οὔτε γὰρ ὅτι ἐν ταῖς ἐνεργείαις ἐπικρατεῖ τὸ θερμὸν ἐν τοῖς ῥόδοις οὔθ’ ὅλως ὅτι τῷ γένει φυτὸν ὑπάρχει, θερμὸν ἤδη διά τι τούτων ἐστίν. οὐ γὰρ οὕτως ἐζητοῦμεν ἡμεῖς τὸ θερμὸν, ἀλλ’ ὡς πρὸς ἄνθρωπον, ἐπεὶ κατά τε τὸ κρατεῖν ἐν ταῖς ἐνεργείαις τὸ θερμὸν ἅπαν φυτὸν ἔσται θερμὸν, ἔτι δὲ μᾶλλον τὰ ζῶα πάντα. καὶ μήκωνα τοίνυν καὶ μανδραγόραν καὶ κώνειον καὶ σαλαμάνδραν θερμὰ λεγόντων ὑπάρχειν, ἀλλ’ οὐ λέγουσιν, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ ἐξ ἑνὸς στόματος ἅπαντες ἀποφαίνονται ψυχρὰ ταῦτ’ εἶναι πάντα. καίτοι ζῶόν τ’ ἐστὶν ἡ σαλαμάνδρα, τρεφόμενον δήπου καὶ αὐξόμενον καὶ κινούμενον, ἅπερ δὴ τοῦ θερμοῦ κρατοῦντος ἐν τῇ κράσει γίνεσθαί φασι. πῶς οὖν ἐν τούτοις μὲν ὡς πρὸς ἄνθρωπον ἀποβλέπουσιν, ἐν ἄλλοις δὲ ὡς πρὸς τὴν ὅλην φύσιν, ἢ τὰς τῶν πρώτων ποιοτήτων ἐνεργείας; ἐγὼ μὲν οὖν φημι μὴ σαλαμάνδραν μόνην, ἢ κώνειον ἀποφαίνεσθαι χρῆναι ψυχρὰ ταῖς δυνάμεσιν ὑπάρχειν, ὡς πρὸς ἄνθρωπον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τἄλλα πάντα κρίνειν ὡσαύτως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For the philosophers, then, inasmuch as they are making pronouncements about the whole of nature, such matters are rightly investigated and stated; but for physicians, who have a different 551 aim, not rightly. For neither because the hot predominates in the activities in roses, nor in general because it is by genus a plant, is it thereby hot on account of any of these things. For we were not inquiring into the hot in that way, but as relative to man; since, by the criterion of the hot's predominating in the activities, every plant will be hot, and still more all the animals. So then let them say that poppy and mandrake and hemlock and salamander are hot; but they do not say so; rather, as if from one mouth, they all declare that all these are cold. And yet the salamander is an animal, one that is surely nourished and grows and moves—things which, they say, come about when the hot predominates in the mixture. How then is it that in these cases they look to the relation to man, but in others to the whole of nature, or to the activities of the primary qualities? I for my part say that one ought not to declare only the salamander or hemlock to be cold in their powers, as relative to man, but to judge all the other things in the same way as well.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐνεργείαις, οὔθ’ ὅλως ὅτι τῷ γένει φυτὸν ὑπάρχει
For the philosophers, then, inasmuch as they are making pronouncements about nature as a whole, such matters are rightly investigated and stated; but for physicians, who have a different 551 aim, not rightly. For neither because the hot predominates in the activities in roses, nor, more generally, because the rose belongs to the genus of plant, is it on that account hot by reason of either of these. For we were not investigating the hot in this sense, but in relation to man, since, if it is a matter of the hot predominating in the activities, every plant will be hot, and all animals still more so. Let them then say that poppy and mandrake and hemlock and salamander are hot. But they do not say so; rather, all of them, as if from one mouth, declare all these things to be cold. And yet the salamander is an animal, one that is surely nourished and grows and moves, which are things they say come about when the hot predominates in the mixture. How, then, is it that in these cases they look to the relation to man, but in others to nature as a whole, or to the activities of the primary qualities? I, for my part, say that one ought to declare not only the salamander or hemlock to be cold in their powers, in relation to man, but also to judge all other things in the same way.

Greek (Kühn)
ἐπεὶ τό γε κώνειον οὐ μόνον οὐ καταψύξει τοὺς ψάρας οὐδ’ ἀναιρεῖ 552 καθάπερ ἡμᾶς, ἀλλὰ καὶ τρέφει καὶ θερμαίνει δηλονότι πᾶν γὰρ τὸ τρέφον οὐσίαν θερμὴν ἐπαύξει δήπου τὸ θερμὸν αὐτῆς καὶ φυλάττει. θερμὴ δέ ἐστιν ἡ τοῦ σώματος οὐσία τῶν ψάρων οὐχ ὡς ζώων μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὡς ἐναίμων. ἐμοὶ μὲν οὖν ἐπιδέδεικται πολλάκις ἐν πολλαῖς ἤδη πραγματείαις ὡς οἰκειότητές τινές εἰσι καὶ μάχαι ποιοτήτων ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς οὖσι, καὶ τὸ μὲν οἰκεῖον ἑτοίμως ἐξομοιοῦται, τὸ δ’ ἐναντίον εἰς φθορὰν ἐνίοτε μεταβάλλει τά τε φυτὰ καὶ τὰ ζῶα. καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ ὡς αἱ οἰκειότητες αὐτῶν κατὰ τὴν ἰδιότητα τῆς ὅλης οὐσίας γίνονται, καὶ τοῦτο εἴρηταί μοι καὶ δέδεικται πολλάκις. οἱ δὲ οὐ δύνανται κατιδεῖν αὐτὸ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ῥᾳθύμως τε ἅμα καὶ ψευδῶς περὶ τῶν πλείστων ἀποφαίνονται φαρμάκων. ἓν γάρ ἐστι μόνον ἀσφαλὲς κριτήριον ἐν αὐτοῖς, ἡ πρὸς ἄνθρωπον πεῖρα, διοριζομένων ἡμῶν ἐν αὐτῇ τὸ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς τοῦ καθ’ αὑτό.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
since hemlock not only does not cool starlings, nor kill them 552 as it does us, but even nourishes them and, evidently, warms them; for everything that nourishes a hot substance surely increases its heat and preserves it. And the substance of the body of starlings is hot not only insofar as they are animals, but also insofar as they are blooded creatures. It has, then, been demonstrated by me many times, in many treatises already, that there are certain affinities and conflicts of qualities in all existing things, and that what is akin is readily assimilated, whereas what is contrary sometimes changes both plants and animals towards destruction. And indeed, that their affinities come about in accordance with the peculiar character of the whole substance—this too has been stated and demonstrated by me many times. But they are unable to discern this, and for that reason they make pronouncements about most drugs carelessly and at the same time falsely. For there is only one safe criterion in their case, the test in relation to a human being, provided that we distinguish in it what is per accidens from what is per se.

For hemlock not only does not chill starlings nor kill 552 them, as it does us, but actually nourishes them and plainly heats them; for everything that nourishes a hot substance surely increases and preserves its heat. And the substance of the body of starlings is hot, not only inasmuch as they are animals but also inasmuch as they are blooded. Now I have shown many times already, in many treatises, that there are certain affinities and conflicts of qualities in all existing things, and that what is akin is readily assimilated, whereas what is contrary sometimes changes both plants and animals to destruction. And indeed I have also stated and demonstrated many times that their affinities arise in accordance with the particular character of the whole substance. But these people are unable to perceive this, and for this reason they make pronouncements about most drugs that are at once careless and false. For in these matters there is only one secure criterion: experience with respect to the human being, provided that in it we distinguish what is accidental from what is per se.

Greek (Kühn)
καὶ ταύτῃ προσέχων τὸν νοῦν οὔτ’ ὄξος, οὔτε ῥόδινον ἐρεῖ τις θερμόν· ψύχει γὰρ ἡμᾶς ἀμφότερα πρώτως καὶ καθ’ ἑαυτὰ, τὸ μὲν ὄξος ἰσχυρῶς, 553 ἐπ’ ὀλίγον δὲ τὸ ῥόδινον. οἷον δέ τι τοῦτ’ ἐστιν τὸ ἐπ’ ὀλίγον, ἐγὼ διηγήσομαι. συγκατοφθήσεται γὰρ αὐτῷ καὶ ὁ περὶ τῆς μέσης κράσεως ἁπάντων τῶν φαρμάκων λόγος, οὗ κᾀν τῷ πρὸ τούτου βιβλίῳ μνήμην ἐποιησάμην ὑπὲρ ἐλαίου διαλεγόμενος. Εἰ δή τι τῶν ὁμιλούντων ἡμῖν σωμάτων ἢ φαρμάκων ἐνεργείᾳ θερμὸν οὕτως ἐστὶν ὡς ἡμεῖς, ἢ γίγνεται πλησιάζον ὁμοίως ἡμῖν θερμὸν, ἐκ τῆς μέσης τὸ τοιοῦτον ὑπάρχει κράσεως, ὥστε ἡμῖν παραβάλλειν, ἐπεί τοι καθ’ ἑαυτὸ δύναιτ’ ἂν κατ’ ἐπικράτειαν λέγεσθαι θερμὸν, ὥσπερ καὶ ἡμεῖς. εἰ δέ τι μήτε ἤδη θερμὸν οὕτως ἐστὶν ὡς ἡμεῖς μήτε ἐν τῷ πλησιάζειν γίνεται τοιοῦτον, ψυχρότερόν ἐστιν ἀνθρώπου κράσεως τοῦτο, κᾂν ὁπωσοῦν ἐν αὐτῷ κρατῇ τὸ θερμόν. καὶ δὴ κᾀν τῷ πλησιάζειν ἡμῖν ἀφαιρήσει τι καὶ καθαιρήσει τῆς ἐνυπαρχούσης ἡμῖν θερμότητος.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συγκατοφθήσεται, παραβάλλειν
And one who attends to this will call neither vinegar nor rose oil hot; for both cool us primarily and in themselves, vinegar strongly, 553 rose oil to a slight degree. What sort of thing this 'to a slight degree' is, I shall explain. For together with it there will also come into view the account concerning the middle mixture of all drugs, of which I made mention in the book before this one as well, when discussing olive oil. If, then, any of the bodies or drugs that come into contact with us is in actuality hot in the same way as we are, or on approaching us becomes hot in like manner to us, such a thing is of the middle mixture, so far as comparing it with us is concerned, since in itself it could be called hot by predominance, just as we too are. But if something is neither already hot in the way we are nor becomes such in approaching us, this is colder than the mixture of a human being, even if in some way or other the hot predominates in it. And indeed, in approaching us, it will take away and diminish something of the heat present in us.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὥστε ἡμῖν παραβάλλειν, ὁμιλούντων, κᾂν ὁπωσοῦν
And if one attends to this, one will call neither vinegar nor rose oil hot; for both cool us primarily and of themselves, the vinegar strongly, 553 the rose oil to a slight degree. What this 'to a slight degree' amounts to, I shall explain; for together with it the account of the middle mixture of all drugs will also come into view, of which I made mention in the book before this one as well, when discussing olive oil. Now, if any of the bodies or drugs that come into contact with us is in actuality hot in the same way as we are, or, on being brought near us, becomes hot in like manner to us, such a thing is of the middle mixture, as compared with us; since, taken of itself, it could be called hot by predominance, just as we too are. And indeed, on being brought near us, it will take away something from the heat present in us and diminish it.

ὥσπερ γὰρ εἰ δυοῖν μὲν ὑδάτοιν ἀμφοῖν μὲν θερμοῖν, ἀλλὰ τοῦ μὲν μᾶλλον, τοῦ δ’ ἧττον, ἐπιμίξαις τῷ μᾶλλον θερμῷ τὸ ἧττον, ἐκλύσεις αὐτοῦ τὴν θερμασίαν, οὕτως εἰ εἰς τὴν ἀπὸ τοῦ ῥοδίνου 554 θερμότητα χλιαρὰν ὑπάρχουσαν καὶ πρὸς τὴν ἀνθρώπου κρᾶσιν ἀναμίξαις ἀνθρωπείαν θερμότητα, καθαιρήσεις τι πάντως αὐτῆς καὶ καταψύξεις. Μὴ τοίνυν θαυμάζειν, εἴ τι τῶν ἰδίᾳ τε καὶ καθ’ αὑτὰ θερμῶν ἑτέρῳ θερμοτέρῳ προσαγόμενον ἐμψύχειν αὐτὸ δύναται. φύσις γὰρ αὕτη κοινὴ πάντων ἐστὶν τῶν ἀλλοιωτῶν σωμάτων, ὡς εἰς ἄδηλα δρᾷν καὶ πάσχειν πλησιάζοντα, τὸ μὲν ἧττον θερμὸν ὑπὸ τοῦ μᾶλλον θερμοῦ θερμότερον γιγνόμενον, τὸ δὲ μᾶλλον θερμὸν ὑπὸ τοῦ ἧττον ἔμπαλιν διατιθέμενον. ἐναργῶς γὰρ αὐτὸ καὶ δι’ αὐτῆς καταμάθοις τῆς ἁφῆς. φαίνεται γὰρ αὐτῇ πολλάκις τὰ αὐτὰ ποτὲ μὲν θερμὰ, ποτὲ δὲ ψυχρά· θερμοτέρᾳ μὲν γιγνομένῃ ψυχρὰ, ψυχροτέρᾳ δὲ θερμά. καί τις ἔναγχος ἡμῖν προὔβαλε διὰ τί ψυχρὸν μὲν ἐν τοῖς βαλανείοις οὐροῦμεν, ἔξω δὲ θερμὸν, οὐ παρακολουθῶν ὅτι τὸ μὲν οὖρον αὐτὸ χλιαρὸν ὁμοίως ἐστὶν ἔν τε τοῖς βαλανείοις κᾀκτὸς, ἡμεῖς δὲ οὐχ ὡσαύτως διακείμεθα τὴν ἐπιφάνειαν τοῦ σώματος ἔν τε τῷ λούεσθαι καὶ πρὸ τούτου.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εἰς ἄδηλα, αὐτῆς (in καθαιρήσεις τι πάντως αὐτῆς)
For just as, if of two waters, both hot, but the one more and the other less, you were to mix the less hot into the more hot, you would slacken its warmth, so if into the heat from rose oil, which is lukewarm even in relation to the mixture of a human being, you were to mix in human heat, you would in every case take away something of it and cool it down. One should not, then, be surprised if something that is hot in its own right and in itself, when brought to another hotter thing, is able to cool it. For this is a nature common to all alterable bodies, that when they come near one another they act and are acted upon to an inapparent degree (εἰς ἄδηλα), the less hot becoming hotter by the agency of the more hot, and the more hot being disposed in the opposite way by the less hot. For you may learn this clearly through touch itself. For to it the same things often appear now hot, now cold: cold when it has become hotter, and hot when it has become colder. And someone recently put to us the problem why we urinate cold in the baths but hot outside, not following that the urine itself is lukewarm in the same way both in the baths and outside, whereas we are not in the same condition with respect to the surface of the body while bathing and before it.

For just as, if of two waters both are hot, but the one more so and the other less, and you mix the less hot into the more hot, you will slacken its warmth, so, if into the heat from rose oil, 554 which is lukewarm even relative to the mixture of a human being, you mix human heat, you will certainly take away something of it and cool it down. You may learn this plainly through touch itself. For the same things often appear to it at one time hot, at another cold: cold when it has become hotter, hot when it has become colder. And someone recently put the question to us why we pass urine that is cold in the baths but hot outside, not following that the urine itself is equally lukewarm both in the baths and outside, whereas we are not in the same condition as regards the surface of the body while bathing and before it.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For while we are bathing we have it [the surface of the body] 555 hotter than the urine, but outside colder; so that with good reason the urine appears to it hot outside the bath, but cold inside. For it has the sensation of what is less hot than itself as of something cooling it down, and of what is hotter than itself as of something heating it up. And it is open to you, for the sake of testing what has been said, when you are already sufficiently warmed while bathing, to order a small pail of moderately lukewarm water to be brought in and to put your hands or your feet into it. For the water will appear to you not lukewarm, but quite cold. But if, immediately upon entering the bath, you were to touch the water in the pail, it will appear less cold to you. For always, to the extent that you have been warmed beforehand, to that extent will it appear cold. And there is nothing surprising in this, if indeed also most well waters, those that have deep springs, appear lukewarm when touched in winter. But if, having bathed, you were to touch them with warm hands, they would not appear lukewarm to you, and much more so if you were still bathing.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀποκαταψύχοντος, καδίσκον, καὶ πολὺ μᾶλλον εἰ λουόμενος ἔτι
For while bathing we have it hotter 555 than the urine is, but outside colder; so that, reasonably, to it the urine too appears hot outside the bath but cold inside. For of what is less hot than itself it has the sensation as of something cooling it down, and of what is hotter than itself as of something heating it through. And you may, to test what has been said, when you have already been sufficiently heated, while bathing order a small tub (kadiskos) of moderately lukewarm water to be brought in, and put your hands or feet into it. For the water will appear to you not lukewarm but considerably cold. But if immediately upon entering the bath you touch the water in the tub, it will appear less cold to you. For always, to whatever degree you have been previously heated, to that degree it will appear cold. And there is nothing surprising in this, since most well waters too, those that have deep sources, when touched in winter appear lukewarm. But if after bathing you touch them with hot hands, they would not appear lukewarm to you, and much less so if you are still bathing.

Greek (Kühn)
γνώμονα τοίνυν ἔχοντες τὴν ἁφὴν ἐν ταῖς τῶν θερμῶν καὶ ψυχρῶν διαγνώσεσι καὶ πρὸς τὴν ἐκείνης διάθεσιν 556 ἀναφέροντες αὐτῶν τὰς κρίσεις εὐλόγως παραλογιζόμεθα πολλάκις, ὡς καὶ τὸ μὴ ψυχρὸν ὅσον ἐφ’ ἑαυτὸ ψυχρὸν εἰπεῖν καὶ τὸ μὴ θερμὸν ὅσον ἐφ’ ἑαυτὸ θερμόν. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο λουόμενοι μὲν ψυχρὸν τὸ οὖρον, ἐῤῥιγωκότες δ’ ἐν κρύει, τὸ φρεατιαῖον ὕδωρ θερμὸν εἶναι νομίζομεν. καὶ εἴπερ τῶν διαθέσεων ἐν αἷς ὄντες ἀμφοτέρων ἐψαύσαμεν ἐπιλαθοίμεθα, μόνης τῆς γεγενημένης ἡμῖν αἰσθήσεως μεμνημένοι, τάχ’ ἀποφηναίμεθα ἄν ποτε τὸ μὲν ὕδωρ ἁπλῶς θερμὸν εἶναι, τὸ δ’ οὖρον ψυχρόν. ἔστι δ’ οὐδέτερον ἁπλῶς οὔτε θερμὸν οὔτε ψυχρὸν, ἀλλ’ ὡς πρὸς ἡμᾶς· καὶ οὐδὲ πρὸς ἡμᾶς αὖ πάλιν ἁπλῶς, ἀλλ’ ὡδί πως ἔχοντας. ἁπλῶς δ’ ἂν πρὸς ἡμᾶς ὁποῖόν ἐστιν ἑκάτερον λέγοιτο πρὸς τὴν μέσην καὶ ἁρίστην ἡμῶν κρᾶσιν ἐξεταζόμενον, ὥσπερ εἰ καὶ πρὸς ἄνθρωπον ἁπλῶς ἐδοκιμάζετο, πάντως ἄν που πρός τε τὸν εὔκρατον ἡ ἐξέτασις ἐγίγνετο. ἤρτηται τοίνυν καὶ ταῦτα πάντα τὰ σφάλματα τῆς τῶν ὀνομάτων διαιρέσεως, ἧς ὀλίγου δεῖν ἅπαντες καταφρονοῦσιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Since, then, we have touch as our criterion in the discriminations of hot and cold things, and refer our judgments of them to the condition of that [faculty], 556 we are, reasonably enough, often misled, so as even to call cold what is not cold in itself, and hot what is not hot in itself. And for this reason, when bathing we think the urine cold, but when we have been shivering in the frost we think the well water hot. And if we should forget the conditions in which we were when we touched both, remembering only the sensation that came to us, we might perhaps at some point declare that the water is simply hot and the urine simply cold. But neither of them is simply either hot or cold, but only in relation to us; and not even in relation to us simply, again, but to us as being in a certain condition. What each of them is simply in relation to us would be stated when it is examined in relation to our middle and best mixture, just as, if it were being tested in relation to man simply, the examination would surely in any case be made in relation to the well-mixed man. All these errors too, then, depend on the division of names, which almost everyone despises.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γνώμονα, ὡδί πως ἔχοντας, πρός τε τὸν εὔκρατον
Since, then, we take touch as our criterion in the discernment of hot and cold things, and refer 556 our judgements about them to its condition, it is reasonable that we are often misled, so as to call what is not cold in itself cold, and what is not hot in itself hot. For this reason, when bathing we think the urine cold, and when we have been chilled in frost we think well-water hot. And if we were to forget the conditions we were in when we touched each of them, remembering only the sensation that arose in us, we might perhaps at some time declare the water to be hot simply and the urine cold. But neither is either hot or cold simply; each is so relative to us. And again, it is not even relative to us simply, but relative to us when we are in some particular state. What each of them is simply relative to us would be stated when it is examined against our middle and best mixture; just as, if it were being tested against man simply, the examination would surely be made against the well-mixed man. All these errors, then, depend on the division of names, which almost everyone despises.

Greek (Kühn)
557 ἐπεὶ γὰρ ψυχρὸν καὶ θερμὸν τὸ μὲν ἁπλῶς λέγεται, τὸ δ’ ἐπικρατείᾳ, τὸ δὲ τῇ πρὸς τὸ σύμμετρον ὁμογενὲς παραβολῇ, τὸ δὲ πρὸς ὁτιοῦν τὸ ἐπιτυχὸν εὐλόγως ἐν ταῖς ὁμωνυμίαις σφαλλόμεθα πολλάκις, ἐπιλανθανόμενοι μὲν ὁποῖόν τι θερμὸν κρίνομεν, ἐφ’ ἕτερον δὲ μεταβαίνοντες. ἀλλὰ μυριάκις ἤδη λέλεκταί τε καὶ λεχθήσεται κατὰ τὸ τέταρτόν τε καὶ ὕστατον τῶν εἰρημένων ἄρτι σημαινομένων ἐπισκέπτεσθαι χρῆναι περὶ τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως. Οὔτε γὰρ ἁπλῶς θερμὸν ἕκαστον αὐτῶν ἐστιν, ὥσπερ τὸ πῦρ· τοῦτο μὲν γὰρ ἂν καὶ ἀδύνατον· οὔτ’ εἰ κατ’ ἐπικράτησιν, ὥσπερ τὸ αἷμα, πρόκειται σκοπεῖν· ἀλλ’ οὐδ’ εἰ πρὸς τὸ σύμμετρον ὁμογενὲς ἢ ὁμοειδὲς λέγεται θερμὸν, ὥσπερ λέων μὲν ἐν ζώοις, τὸ δ’ ἀκμάζον ἐν ἑκάστῳ τῶν εἰδῶν πρὸς τὸ γεγηρακός. ἀλλ’ εἰ πρὸς ἄνθρωπόν ἐστι θερμὸν, ἢ ψυχρὸν, ἢ ξηρὸν, ἢ ὑγρὸν ἕκαστον τῶν φαρμάκων, ἡ ζήτησίς ἐστιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
557 For since 'cold' and 'hot' are said in one sense simply, in another by predominance, in another by comparison with the well-proportioned thing of the same genus, and in another in relation to anything whatever that happens to be at hand, we reasonably go wrong often amid the homonymies, forgetting what sort of hot thing we are judging and passing over to another. But it has already been said countless times, and will be said again, that one must investigate the power of drugs according to the fourth and last of the significations just mentioned. For neither is each of them hot simply, as fire is—for that would indeed be impossible—nor is it proposed to examine whether it is hot by predominance, as blood is; nor yet whether it is called hot in relation to the well-proportioned thing of the same genus or of the same species, as a lion is among animals, and as that which is in its prime, within each of the species, is in relation to that which has grown old. Rather, the inquiry is whether each of the drugs is hot, or cold, or dry, or moist in relation to a human being.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σύμμετρον

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐν ἴσῳ τὸ ψυκτικὸν εἶναι ἀνθρώπου φύσεως
and not even in relation to him as something compared in the manner of a relative, but whether it is so in power — that 558 is, whether, when brought near to him, it renders the part which it touches hotter, or colder, or moister, or drier. So that both the matter of judgement and that of discrimination will lie in experience, which most physicians abandon, inquiring instead whether the rose is red or fragrant, and neglecting, when they have been overheated in the head, to put on it garlands of roses, or their juice, or rose oil, and so to learn by experience what happens. For if they are heated up, it would rather be hot; but if they are cooled, cold — which is the same as saying that it is cooling in relation to human nature; for one must always keep this in mind, although I never cease saying it countless times, while most people mishear it, and one must pardon them, perverted as they are by sophists who talk nonsense at length. The matters of experience, then, are thus easy, both concerning the power of roses and concerning all the other drugs.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς τὸ πρός τι παραβαλλόμενον, τῇ δυνάμει, διαγνώσεως, παρέντες ... ἐπιτιθέντες (construction apparently corrupt; infinitive expected after παρέντες), ἐγκαυθῶσι τὴν κεφαλὴν, ἐν ἴσῳ τὸ ψυκτικὸν εἶναι ἀνθρώπου φύσεως
And not even in relation to him in the way that what is compared falls under the category of relation, but whether it is so potentially; that 558 is, whether, when brought near to him, it renders the part that it touches hotter, or colder, or moister, or drier. So that both the matter of judgement and the matter of discernment will lie in experience. Most physicians abandon this and instead examine whether the rose is red or fragrant. When they have been scorched by heat on the head, they neglect to place upon it garlands of roses, or their juice, or rose oil, and so to learn by experience what happens. For if they are heated through, it would be rather hot; but if they are cooled, cold. Here 'cold' is equivalent to being cooling of the nature of man, for one must always remember this. Yet although I keep saying it countless times, most people mishear it, and one must forgive them, perverted as they have been by the sophists who drivel at length. The matters of experience, then, are this easy, both concerning the power of roses and concerning all the other drugs.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For it is surely possible both to crown oneself with roses, and, having at some time pounded them, to apply them as a plaster to the mouth of the stomach of a person burning with fever, and 559 having pressed out their juice, to gulp it down, and having mixed it with water or with oil, to rub some one of the parts of the body with it. And yet why do I say this to these people, when in fact some people, mixing rose juice with opobalsam, rub their bodies with it in the burning heats under the Dog-star for the sake of cooling, having learned by experience itself that they cool sufficiently? But out of excessive wisdom Herodotus the physician does not even admit that rose oil with vinegar, which they specifically call oxyrhodinon, cools us; then, having recalled at least this much, that he himself uses it in the beginnings of phrenitic diseases, and being aware of the contradiction—for it is agreed by almost all physicians, even down to the followers of the most senseless Thessalus, that at the beginning one must repel and cool rather than warm and relax—he says that it does not cool but is astringent. And yet there was nothing difficult in his too, when he himself was at some time overheated by the sun—for he was a human being subject to the same affections as we are—moistening his head with oxyrhodinon and coming to know by experience the activity of the drug.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καυσουμένου, ἐκροφῆσαι
For it is surely possible both to crown oneself with roses, and at some time, having pounded them, to apply them as a poultice to the mouth of the stomach of a person suffering from burning fever, and, 559 having squeezed out their juice, to sip it down, and, having mixed it with water or oil, to rub some one of the parts of the body with it. And yet why do I speak of these things, when some people, mixing juice of roses with balsam sap, rub down their bodies during the heats under the Dog-star for the sake of cooling, having learned by experience itself that they cool sufficiently? Then, remembering at least this much, that he himself uses it at the beginnings of phrenitic diseases, and perceiving the contradiction (for it is agreed by nearly all physicians, down even to the followers of the most insensate Thessalus, that at the beginning one must repel and cool rather than heat and relax), he says that it does not cool but is astringent. And yet there would have been no difficulty, when he too had at some time been overheated (for he was a man subject to the same affections as we are), in moistening his head with oxyrhodinon and learning by experience the action of the drug.

﻿ 560 ῥᾷστον δ’ ἦν, οἶμαι, καὶ ψυγέντα χρῆσθαί ποτ’ αὐτῷ κατὰ τε τῆς κεφαλῆς ἐπιχέοντα καὶ τοῖς ὠσὶν ἐνστάξαντα καὶ ταῖς ῥισὶν ἐγχέοντα καὶ τὸ μέτωπον ὅλον ἀνατρίψαντα, καθάπερ ἀμαρακίνῳ καὶ ἰρίνῳ καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις ἀλείμμασιν τοῖς θερμαίνουσι χρώμεθα. καίτοι γὰρ οὐδὲ μετρίως θερμαίνει κατ’ αὐτὸν ὀξυρόδινον, ἐκ δυοῖν θερμοτάτων φαρμάκων συγκείμενον. ἀλλ’ εἴπερ Ἡρόδοτος οὐκ ἐτόλμησε χρήσασθαι, δίκαιον, οἶμαι, τοὺς νῦν ἐστι πειραθῆναι τῆς τοῦ φαρμάκου δυνάμεως ἐφ’ ἑαυτῶν, ὅταν ἤτοι ψυγῶσιν ἢ ἐγκαυθῶσιν, ὥσπερ ἐγὼ πολλάκις ἐπειράθην ἐπ’ ἐμαυτοῦ καὶ ἄλλων οὐκ ὀλίγων ἐγκεκαυμένων ἐπιβρέξας τὴν κεφαλήν. ἄμεινον γάρ ἐστιν αὐτοὺς πρώτως ὧν λέγουσι πειραθέντας οὕτως ἡμῖν τε συμβουλεύειν ἔν τε ταῖς βίβλοις ἀναγράφειν. Ἀλλὰ γὰρ οὐκ οἶδ’ ὅπως, καίτοι φυλαττόμενος ἀντιλογίας ἅψασθαι, πρὸς τῆς ἀναισχυντίας αὐτῶν ἠναγκάσθην.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
560 And it would have been very easy, I think, also when chilled to use it at some time, pouring it over the head, instilling it into the ears, pouring it into the nostrils, and rubbing the whole forehead with it, just as we use marjoram-oil and iris-oil and the other warming unguents. For indeed, according to him, oxyrhodinon heats not even moderately, being composed of two very hot drugs. But if Herodotus did not dare to use it, it is right, I think, that those of the present day should make trial of the power of the drug on themselves, whenever they have either been chilled or been scorched by the sun, just as I have often made trial of it on myself and on not a few others who had been scorched, having moistened the head with it. For it is better that they should first themselves have made trial of what they say, and so advise us and record it in their books. But indeed, I know not how, although guarding against engaging in controversy, I have been compelled to it by their shamelessness.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καίτοι γὰρ οὐδὲ μετρίως θερμαίνει κατ’ αὐτὸν, δίκαιον, οἶμαι, τοὺς νῦν ἐστι
560 And it would have been very easy, I think, for him also to use it at some time when he had been chilled, pouring it over the head, dropping it into the ears, pouring it into the nostrils and rubbing it over the whole forehead, just as we use marjoram oil and iris oil and the other warming unguents. For indeed, by his account, oxyrhodinum heats in no merely moderate degree, being compounded of two very hot drugs. But if Herodotus did not dare to use it, it is right, I think, for those now living to test the power of the drug on themselves, whenever they have been either chilled or overheated, as I have often tested it on myself and on not a few others who were overheated, moistening the head with it. For it is better that they themselves first test the things they speak of, and only then advise us and record them in their books. But somehow, I know not how, although I was guarding against taking up controversy, I have been compelled to it by their shamelessness.

Greek (Kühn)
αὖθις οὖν ἐπάνειμι πάλιν ἐφ’ ὅπερ ἐξ ἀρχῆς προὐθέμην, ὡς τῇ πείρᾳ κριτέον ἐστὶ τὰς τῶν φαρμάκων 561 δυνάμεις, ἐπί τε τῶν ὑγιαινόντων ἀμέμπτως χρώμενον ἐπί τε τῶν νοσούντων ἁπλᾶς ὡς ἔνι μάλιστα νόσους ἤτοι θερμὰς, ἢ ψυχρὰς, ἢ ξηρὰς, ἢ ὑγράς. οὕτω γάρ σοι κρίνοντι τὸ μὲν ἔλαιον οὔτε θερμὸν οὔτε ψυχρὸν ὡς πρὸς ἄνθρωπον εὔκρατον ἑρεθήσεται, τὸ δὲ ῥόδινον ἤδη μέν πω ψυχρότερον, οὐ μὴν ἰσχυρῶς γε ψυχρὸν, ἀλλ’ ὡς ὀνομάζειν εἴωθα τῆς πρώτης τῶν ψυχόντων ἀποστάσεώς τε καὶ τάξεως. ὥσπερ γὰρ ὕδωρ, εἰ τύχοι, τὸ μὲν ἀκριβῶς ἐστιν εὔκρατον, τὸ δ’ ἤτοι θερμότερον ἢ ψυχρότερον, οὐ μὴν ἤδη γέ πως θερμὸν ἢ ψυ χρὸν ἀπολελυμένως εἰπεῖν, οὕτω κᾀν τοῖς φαρμάκοις ἔλαιον μὲν ἐκ τῆς μέσης ὑπάρχει κράσεως, ὁ δὲ τῶν ῥόδων χυλὸς ἐκ τῆς ψυχροτέρας μὲν, ἀλλ’ οὐ πολλῷ τινι κατὰ τὴν χλιαρὰν, ὡς εἴρηταί μοι καὶ πρόσθεν, ὑπάρχων θερμασίαν. ἐκ δὲ τῆς αὐτῆς ἐστιν αὐτῷ τάξεως τὸ καλούμενον λινόσπερμον ὅσον ἐπὶ τῷ χλιαρῷ τῆς θερμασίας, ἀλλὰ τῷ παχυμερὲς ὑπάρχειν οὐκ ὀλίγῳ δή τινι παραλλάττεται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἑρεθήσεται
So I return once more to what I set out from the beginning, namely that the powers of drugs 561 must be judged by experience, using them both on those who are in blameless health and on those suffering diseases as simple as possible, whether hot, or cold, or dry, or moist. For if you judge in this way, olive oil will be found neither hot nor cold relative to a well-tempered human being, while rose oil will be found already somewhat colder, yet certainly not strongly cold, but, as I am accustomed to name it, of the first distance and order of the cooling things. For just as water, for instance, is in one case precisely well-tempered, in another either warmer or colder, yet not thereby in any way hot or cold, to speak absolutely, so too among drugs olive oil belongs to the middle mixture, whereas the juice of roses belongs to the colder one, though not by any great amount, being, as I have also said before, at lukewarm warmth. And of the same order as it is what is called linseed, so far as concerns the lukewarmness of its warmth, but by being coarse-particled it differs to no small degree.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀμέμπτως χρώμενον, κατὰ τὴν χλιαρὰν ... θερμασίαν
So I return once again to what I proposed at the outset: that the powers of drugs 561 must be judged by experience, using them irreproachably on those who are healthy, and on those who are sick with diseases as simple as possible, either hot, or cold, or dry, or moist. For if you judge in this way, oil will be said to be neither hot nor cold in relation to a well-tempered human being, while rose oil is already somewhat colder, yet not strongly cold, but, as I am accustomed to name it, of the first distance and order of cooling things. For just as with water, say, one is exactly well-tempered while another is either warmer or colder, yet not such that one could already call it hot or cold absolutely, so too among drugs oil is of the middle mixture, whereas the juice of roses is of the colder, though not by any great amount, being, as I have also said before, of lukewarm warmth. Of the same order as it is what is called linseed, so far as the lukewarm quality of its warmth is concerned, but by being coarse-particled it differs from it by no small amount.

K11.561-562K. XI 561–562, ch. 3.10 difficulty mid (1.88/100; thin 2, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 8/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
λεπτομερὴς γὰρ ἀκριβῶς ἐστιν ὁ τῶν ῥόδων χυλὸς, ὡς δηλοῖ 562 τό τε ξηραίνεσθαι ῥᾳδίως αὐτὸν καὶ τὸ μηδεμιᾶς μετέχειν γλισχρότητος καὶ τὸ τὰς τῶν ἄλλως μύρων ἐκλύειν ὀσμάς. οὐδὲ γὰρ οὐδὲ τοῦτο ἑτέρῳ τινὶ φαίνεται ποιεῖν ἢ τῷ φθάνειν ἐμπιπλάναι τοὺς ὀσφρητικοὺς πόρους. ἐπειδὴ γὰρ μελλόντων διϊέναι καὶ διαδύεσθαι τῶν ἄλλων αὐτοὺς προκαταλαμβάνει τε καὶ πληροῖ. ἔστι δὲ καὶ τὸ χαμαίμηλον, ὅσον μὲν ἐπὶ λεπτομερείᾳ, ῥόδῳ παραπλήσιον, ὅσον δ’ ἐπὶ θερμότητι, τῆς ἐλαίου μᾶλλον δυνάμεως, οἰκείας τε τῷ ζώῳ καὶ συμμέτρου, διὸ καὶ κόπου ἀρωγόν ἐστιν εἴπερ τι καὶ ἄλλο καὶ ἀλγημάτων πραϋντικὸν, ἀνίησί τε καὶ χαλᾷ τὰ τεταμένα καὶ μαλάττει τὰ μετρίως σκληρὰ καὶ ἀραιοῖ τὰ πεπυκνωμένα. καὶ πυρετῶν, ὁπόσοι χωρὶς σπλάγχνου φλεγμονῆς ἐνοχλοῦσι, λυτικὸν ὑπάρχει, καὶ τούτων μάλιστα τῶν ἐπὶ χολώδεσι χυμοῖς ἢ πυκνώσει δέρματος συνισταμένων. ταύτῃ τοι καὶ τῶν Αἰγυπτίων τοῖς σοφωτάτοις ἡλίῳ τε καθιέρωται καὶ πυρετῶν ἁπάντων ἴαμα νενόμισται. ἀλλὰ ἐν τούτῳ μὲν οὐκ ἀληθεύουσιν. μόνον γὰρ ὧν εἴρηκα πυρετῶν ἐστιν ἴαμα καὶ τούτων ἤδη πεττομένων.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄλλως (μύρων), ἐπειδὴ γὰρ
For the juice of roses is precisely fine-particled, as is shown 562 by its drying easily, by its partaking of no viscosity, and by its dissolving the odours of the other perfumes. For it appears to do this too by nothing other than by being the first to fill up the olfactory passages; for when the others are about to pass through and penetrate, it occupies them beforehand and fills them. Chamomile too is, as regards fineness of parts, similar to the rose, but as regards heat it is rather of the power of olive oil, which is proper to the animal and well-proportioned; wherefore it is also a help against fatigue, if anything is, and soothing of pains, and it relaxes and slackens what is tense, softens what is moderately hard, and rarefies what has been condensed. And it is resolvent of fevers, as many as cause trouble without inflammation of a viscus, and of these especially those which arise from bilious humours or from condensation of the skin. For this reason, indeed, among the wisest of the Egyptians it has been consecrated to the sun and held to be a remedy for all fevers. But in this they do not speak the truth; for it is a remedy only for the fevers I have mentioned, and of these when they are already being concocted.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἄλλως (in τῶν ἄλλως μύρων; probably to be read ἄλλων, 'the other perfumes'), ἐπειδὴ γὰρ (syntax incomplete; rendered as 'for when'), ἐκλύειν (rendered 'dispelling'; literally 'loosening, weakening'), λυτικὸν (rendered 'resolvent', in the sense of bringing fevers to an end)
For the juice of roses is precisely fine-particled, as is shown 562 by its drying easily, by its having no share of viscidity, and by its dispelling the odours of the other perfumes. For it is evident that it does this too by nothing other than by being first to fill the olfactory passages: for when the others are about to pass through and slip into them, it occupies them beforehand and fills them. Chamomile too, as far as fineness of particles goes, is close to the rose, but as far as heat goes, it is rather of the power of oil, a power that is proper to the animal and well-proportioned. For this reason it is a help against fatigue if anything else is, and soothing of pains; it relaxes and loosens what is tensed, softens what is moderately hard, and rarefies what has been condensed. And it is resolvent of fevers, of as many as trouble the patient without inflammation of a viscus, and most of all of those that arise from bilious humours or from condensation of the skin. It is for this very reason that among the Egyptians the wisest have consecrated it to the sun and have held it to be a remedy for all fevers. But in this they are not right. For it is a remedy only for the fevers I have mentioned, and for these only when they are already being concocted.

K11.562-563K. XI 562–563, ch. 3.10 difficulty mid (2.08/100; thin 0, absent 3) (a) fail (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ὀνίνησι μέντοι καλῶς καὶ τοὺς 563 ἄλλους ἅπαντας, ὅσοι τε μελαγχολικοὶ καὶ ὅσοι φλεγματώδεις εἰσὶ καὶ σπλάγχνου φλεγμονῆς ἔκγονοι. καὶ γὰρ καὶ τούτων ἴαμα γενναιότατόν ἐστιν τὸ χαμαίμηλον, ὅταν ἤδη πεττομένοις προσφέρηται, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ ὑποχονδρίοις εὐμενὲς εἴπερ τι ἄλλο. καὶ τὸ λινόσπερμον γοῦν τοιοῦτον ὁ παλαιὸς λόγος εἶναί φησι. καὶ γάρ ἐστιν ὄντως κᾀκεῖνο τοῖς καθ’ ὑποχόνδρια σπλάγχνοις εὐμενὲς, ἀλλ’ ἧττον χαμαιμήλου. καὶ γὰρ καὶ θερμὸν ἧττόν ἐστιν, ὡς εἴρηται, καὶ πρὸς τούτῳ καὶ παχυμερὲς ὑπάρχει, οὐ μὴν τό γε ῥόδον ἐγγὺς αὐτοῖς εἰς ταῦτα, καίτοι γ’ ὅσον ἐπί τε τὸ χλιαρὸν τῆς θερμότητος καὶ τῷ λεπτομερεῖ, χρήσιμον ἂν ἦν ἱκανῶς καὶ τοῦτο ταῖς καθ’ ὑποχόνδρια φλεγμοναῖς, ἀλλ’ ἡ συνοῦσα στύψις αὐτῷ πολλαχῆ παραβλάπτει καὶ μάλιστ’ ἐν οἷς εὐδόκιμόν ἐστι χαμαίμηλον, ἢ τὰ πεπυκνωμένα τῶν σωμάτων ἀραιοῦν, ἢ τὰ κατεσκληρυμμένα μαλάττον, ἢ τὰ τεταμένα χαλῶν. εἰς γὰρ τὰ τοιαῦτα πάντα πολέμιον ἡ στύψις.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
It does, however, benefit well also all the 563 others, both those that are melancholic and those that are phlegmatic and the offspring of inflammation of a viscus. For chamomile is a most noble remedy of these too, whenever it is applied to them once they are already being concocted, and on this account it is also kindly to the hypochondria if anything is. And linseed too, at any rate, the ancient account says is of this sort. And indeed that too is really kindly to the viscera in the hypochondria, but less so than chamomile. For it is both less hot, as has been said, and in addition to this it is coarse-particled; the rose, however, does not come close to them in these respects, although, as far as concerns the lukewarmness of its heat and its fine-particled quality, this too would have been sufficiently useful for inflammations in the hypochondria; but the astringency that accompanies it does harm in many ways, and especially in those cases in which chamomile is of good repute, either rarefying the condensed parts of bodies, or softening the hardened, or relaxing the tense. For astringency is hostile to all such things.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γενναιότατόν, ὁ παλαιὸς λόγος, ἐπί τε τὸ χλιαρὸν τῆς θερμότητος καὶ τῷ λεπτομερεῖ
It does, however, also benefit well all the 563 other fevers, both those that are melancholic and those that are phlegmatic and those that are the offspring of inflammation of an internal organ. For chamomile is a most excellent (γενναιότατον) remedy for these too, when it is administered to them when they are already being concocted; and for this reason it is also kindly to the hypochondria, if anything else is. Linseed, at any rate, the old account (ὁ παλαιὸς λόγος) states to be of this kind as well. And indeed that too really is kindly to the internal organs in the region of the hypochondria, but less so than chamomile. For it is both less hot, as has been said, and in addition to this it is coarse-particled. The rose, however, does not come near them for these purposes. And yet, as far as the lukewarm character of its heat and its fine-particled nature are concerned, it too would be quite useful for inflammations in the region of the hypochondria; but the astringency present in it does harm in many ways. It does so especially in those things in which chamomile is highly esteemed: either rarefying the condensed parts of bodies, or softening those that have been hardened, or relaxing those that are stretched tight. For astringency is hostile to all such things.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For the onsets and increases of inflammations, however, and especially 564 of superficial ones, rose oil is better than chamomile, because these require both a lukewarm warmth and a slight astringency. For at the beginning cooling and astringent things are useful, during the increases those which are lukewarm and moderately astringent, and at the peaks of inflammations those which warm more, in due measure, and disperse strongly. All these matters will be discussed at greater length in the books of the Therapeutic Method. For now I return again to the subject at hand. For indeed the drugs of the middle mixture, and those which deviate slightly toward either side, may seem at one time to warm and at another in turn to cool, and sometimes neither to warm nor to cool but to preserve the bodies in the condition in which they received them; and this happens to them with good reason, if indeed lukewarm water and urine appear cold in the baths, but warm to those who have been chilled.

εἴπερ οὖν παρὰ τό πως ἔχειν τὰ διατιθέμενα καὶ τὸ διατιθὲν οὐχ ὁμοίως ἐνεργεῖν φαίνεται, δῆλον ὡς οὔτε τὸ ῥόδον οὔτε τοὔλαιον οὔτε τὸ χαμαίμηλον ﻿ 565 οὔτε τὸ λινόσπερμον, ἀλλ’ οὐδὲ τὸ ἄνηθον οὔτε τὸ τήλινον ἄλευρον, ἀλλ’ οὐδὲ τὸ πύρινον οὐδ’ ἄλλα μυρία, τά τε ἀκριβῶς μέσα ταῖς κράσεσιν, τά τε βραχὺ παραλλάττοντα, τὴν αὐτὴν ἐνέργειαν ἐπιδείξεται διὰ παντός. οὔτε γὰρ ὑγιαίνοντες ὁμοίως κεκράμεθα πάντες, οὔτε τὰς αὐτὰς νοσοῦμεν νόσους. αὐτίκα γέ τοι τὸ ῥόδινον ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ τὴν κρᾶσιν κεκραμένον ἐλαίου τε καὶ τοῦ τῶν ῥόδων χυλοῦ. τοῦ μὲν γὰρ ἧττον ὑπάρχει θερμὸν, τοῦ δὲ μᾶλλον. ἀναψύχει μὲν γὰρ τοὺς ἐγκεκαυμένους τῷ χλιαρῷ δηλονότι τῆς θερμότητος, ὀλίγον δέ τι καὶ τοὺς κατεψυγμένους θερμαίνει. διότι καὶ τοῦτο ἔργον ἐστὶ χλιαρᾶς θερμασίας, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τὰ βαλανεῖα καὶ τοὺς ῥιγῶντας θερμαίνει καὶ τοὺς ἐγκεκαυμένους ἀναψύχει. Καὶ διὰ τοῦτο κᾀν τοῖς ψύχειν ἢ θερμαίνειν πεφυκόσι φαρμάκοις, ἐὰν ἀναμίξῃς τὸ ῥόδινον, οὐδὲν ἐργάσῃ μέγα κακὸν, ὥσπερ δ’ ἀξιόλογον ἀγαθὸν οὐδέν. ἡ γὰρ χλιαρὰ θερμασία βλάπτειν μὲν οὐκ ἔστιν ἱκανὴ τὰ κατεψυγμένα, θεραπεύειν δ’ οὐ δύναται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
If, then, in accordance with the condition of the things being affected, the agent too appears not to act in the same way, it is clear that neither the rose nor the oil nor chamomile 565 nor linseed, nor indeed dill nor fenugreek flour, nor wheat flour either, nor countless others—both those that are exactly intermediate in their mixtures and those that depart slightly from it—will display the same activity throughout. For neither are we all mixed alike when we are healthy, nor do we fall ill with the same diseases. To take an immediate example, rose oil is, in its mixture, mixed intermediately between oil and the juice of roses; for it is less hot than the one and more so than the other. For it cools those who are burning, obviously by the lukewarmness of its heat, while it also warms a little those who have been chilled, since this too is a work of lukewarm warmth, just as baths too both warm those who are shivering and cool those who are burning. And for this reason, even if you mix rose oil into drugs whose nature it is to cool or to heat, you will do no great harm, just as you will do no notable good either. For lukewarm warmth is not capable of harming parts that have been chilled, but neither is it able to cure them.

If, then, it is evident that the agent which affects, too, does not act in the same way according to the condition of the things being affected, it is clear that neither the rose nor olive oil nor chamomile 565 nor linseed, nor yet dill nor fenugreek meal, nor yet wheaten meal nor countless others, both those exactly intermediate in their mixtures and those that deviate slightly, will display the same activity in every case. For neither are we all mixed alike when healthy, nor do we suffer from the same diseases. Rose oil, for instance, is mixed with a mixture intermediate between olive oil and the juice of roses: for it is less hot than the former and hotter than the latter. It cools again those who are overheated, evidently by the lukewarm character of its heat, and it also warms to some small degree those who are chilled. This too is the work of lukewarm warmth, just as the baths both warm those who are shivering and cool again those who are overheated. And for this reason, if you mix rose oil into drugs that are naturally disposed to cool or to warm, you will do no great harm, just as you will do no noteworthy good either. For lukewarm warmth is not capable of harming things that have been chilled, yet it cannot cure them.

K11.565-566K. XI 565–566, ch. 3.12 difficulty mid (1.97/100; thin 1, absent 2) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 15/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
δεῖται γὰρ τὸ 566 θεραπευόμενον ἅπαν, ὡς ἂν ἐξ ἀμετρίας τινὸς εἰς συμμετρίαν ἀγόμενον, οὐκ αὐτοῦ τοῦ συμμέτρου τὴν κρᾶσιν, ἀλλὰ τῆς ἐναντίας ἀμετρίας. εἰ γοῦν τὸ ψυχρὸν ὕδωρ βουληθείης εὔκρατον ποιῆσαι, τὸ μὲν εὔκρατον ἤδη μιγνύειν οὐ χρὴ χλιαρὸν γὰρ οὕτως ἔσται τὸ μικτὸν ἐξ ἀμφοῖν. ἔρχεσθαι δ’ ἐπὶ τὸ ζέον προσήκει καὶ τοῦτο μιγνύναι τῷ ψυχρῷ. καὶ τοσούτῳ γε θερμότερον εἶναι χρὴ τοῦ συμμέτρου τὸ τῷ ψυχρῷ μιγνύμενον, ὅσῳ περ ἂν καὶ τὸ ψυχρὸν αὐτὸ ψυχρότερον ἦν τοῦ συμμέτρου. οὔτε γὰρ τὸ βραχὺ τοῦ συμμέτρου ψυχρότερον ὑπὸ τοῦ πολὺ θερμοτέρου τὴν μέσην ἀπολήψεται κρᾶσιν, οὔτε τὸ πολλῷ ψυχρότερον ὑπὸ τοῦ βραχὺ θερμοτέρου. ἀλλ’ εἴτε πολλῷ ψυχρότερόν ἐστι τὸ τῆς θεραπείας δεόμενον, ἀνάγκη καὶ τὸ θεραπεῦσον αὐτὸ πολλῷ θερμότερον ὑπάρχειν, εἴτ’ ὀλίγῳ ψυχρότερον, ὀλίγῳ χρὴ καὶ τὸ θεραπεῦον εἶναι θερμότερον. ἑνὶ δὲ λόγῳ τοσοῦτον ἀπέχειν χρὴ τοῦ μέσου τὸ θεραπεῦσον, ὅσον περ καὶ τὸ θεραπευθησόμενον, ἐπὶ τἀναντία δηλονότι τῆς ἀποστάσεως ὑπαρχούσης ἑκατέρῳ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For everything that is being treated, 566 inasmuch as it is being brought from some excess into due proportion, needs not what is itself moderate in its mixture, but the opposite excess. At any rate, if you should wish to make cold water well-tempered, you must not mix in what is already well-tempered; for in that way the mixture of both will be lukewarm. Rather, it is fitting to go to boiling water and to mix this with the cold. And indeed what is mixed with the cold must be hotter than the moderate by just as much as the cold itself was colder than the moderate. For neither will what is slightly colder than the moderate recover the middle mixture by means of what is much hotter, nor will what is much colder do so by means of what is slightly hotter. But if what needs treatment is much colder, it is necessary that what is to treat it also be much hotter; and if it is slightly colder, what treats it must also be slightly hotter. In a word, what is to treat must stand as far from the middle as what is to be treated, the distance of each lying, obviously, in opposite directions.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
For everything that is 566 being treated, inasmuch as it is being brought from some immoderation into due proportion, needs not the mixture of the moderate itself but that of the opposite immoderation. So if you should wish to make cold water well-tempered, you must not mix in water that is already well-tempered, for in that way the mixture made from both will be lukewarm. Rather one ought to go to boiling water and mix this with the cold. And what is mixed with the cold must be hotter than the moderate by just as much as the cold itself was colder than the moderate. For neither will what is slightly colder than the moderate recover the middle mixture through what is much hotter, nor what is much colder through what is slightly hotter. But if what needs treatment is much colder, then what is to treat it must necessarily also be much hotter; and if it is a little colder, then what treats it must also be a little hotter. In a word, what is to treat must stand as far from the middle as does what is to be treated, the departure of each evidently lying in opposite directions.

Greek (Kühn)
τὸ γὰρ ἐπὶ τὰ αὐτὰ παραπλησίως 567 ἀφεστηκὸς, ὅμοιον τῇ κράσει τῷ θεραπευομένῳ καθεστηκὸς, οὐδεμίαν ἀλλοίωσιν ἐπιφέρειν ἱκανὸν, ὥστε οὔτε βλάψει τι παθὸν οὔτ’ ὠφελήσει. τὸ δ’ ἔλαττον ὠφελήσει μὲν, οὐ μὴν ἰάσεταί γε πάντως. εἰ τοίνυν τὸ μὲν πάθος εἴη ψυχρὸν, ἡ δὲ τοῦ φαρμάκου θερμότης χλιαρὰ, πάντως μέν τι προσωφελήσει τὸ πάθος, οὐ μὴν εἰς τὴν κατὰ φύσιν γε πάντως ἐπανήξει συμμετρίαν, ὥστ’ οὐδὲν ἐξιάσεται. πάλιν δὲ εἰ μὲν τὸ πάθος εἴη μικρὸν, ὡς ἂν ἀπὸ τῆς συμμέτρου θερμασίας τῆς κατὰ φύσιν εἰς οἷον χλιαράν τινα μεταπεπτωκέναι, τὸ δὲ φάρμακον εἴη ψυχρὸν, ἀνάγκη ἐπιψυγῆναί τε καὶ βλαβῆναι τὸ πάθος ὑπὸ τοῦ ψυχροῦ. τὸ δ’ αὐτὸ κᾂν θερμότερον ᾖ τῆς συμμετρίας τὸ πάθος, ὑπὸ μὲν τῶν ὁμοίων αὐτοῦ φαρμάκων ἐν ταὐτῷ φυλαχθήσεται, βλαβήσεται δὲ ὑπὸ τῶν ἧττον ἑαυτῶν θερμῶν, οὐ μὴν ἐκθεραπευθήσεταί γε πάντως. ἥκει δὴ κᾀνταῦθα πάλιν ὁμωνυμία τις εἰς ὅλον ἐκτεταμένη τὸν βίον καὶ πολλαχόθι σφάλλουσα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βλαβήσεται δὲ ὑπὸ τῶν ἧττον ἑαυτῶν θερμῶν, τι παθὸν
For that which stands at a similar distance in the same direction, being similar in mixture to what is being treated, is capable of bringing about no alteration, so that it will neither harm anything affected nor benefit it. That which is less [distant] will indeed benefit, yet it will certainly not cure. If, then, the affection should be cold, while the heat of the drug is lukewarm, it will certainly help the affection somewhat, yet it will not in every case bring it back to the natural proportion, so that it will not cure it at all. Again, if the affection should be small, such that it has shifted from the moderate warmth that is according to nature to, as it were, a kind of lukewarm warmth, while the drug is cold, the affection must necessarily be chilled and harmed by the cold. The same holds even if the affection is hotter than the proportionate state: by drugs similar to it, it will be kept in the same state, but it will be harmed by those less hot than themselves (ὑπὸ τῶν ἧττον ἑαυτῶν θερμῶν), yet it will certainly not be fully cured. Here too, then, there arrives once again a certain homonymy, extended over the whole of life and leading astray in many places.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τι παθὸν, βλαβήσεται δὲ ὑπὸ τῶν ἧττον ἑαυτῶν θερμῶν, ἐξιάσεται
For what is, to a comparable degree, 567 removed in the same direction, being similar in mixture to what is being treated, is capable of bringing about no alteration, so that it will neither harm anything that has been affected (τι παθὸν) nor benefit it. What is less removed will benefit, yet it will certainly not cure. If, then, the affection is cold and the heat of the drug lukewarm, it will certainly confer some additional benefit on the affection, yet it will certainly not bring it back to the natural due proportion, so that it will cure it completely in no respect. Again, if the affection is small, so as to have shifted from the moderate natural warmth to a sort of lukewarm one, and the drug is cold, the affection must necessarily be further chilled and harmed by the cold. The same holds if the affection is hotter than due proportion: by drugs similar to it, it will be kept in the same state, and it will be harmed by those less hot than themselves (ἧττον ἑαυτῶν θερμῶν, so the text), yet it will certainly not be thoroughly cured. Here too, then, there arises again a certain homonymy, extended over the whole of life and causing error in many places.
