καὶ γὰρ τὸ συμμέτρως 568 κεκραμένον ὀνομάζομεν ἐνίοτε θερμὸν, ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ βαλανεῖον τὸ τοιοῦτον καὶ τὸ πόμα καὶ τὸ τούτου λειπόμενον οὐ πολλῷ, καὶ πρὸς τούτοις ἔτι τὸ θερμότερον ἅπαν. εἰ δέ γε τοῖς ὀνόμασιν ἀκριβῶς ἐχρώ μεθα, τὸ μὲν σύμμετρον οὐ θερμὸν, ἀλλ’ εὔκρατον ἂν ἐκαλοῦμεν. ὅσον δ’ ἂν ἀμετρότερον τούτου, τὸ μὲν ἐπὶ τὸ θερμότερον ἄμετρον ἧττον τε καὶ μᾶλλον θερμὸν, τὸ δ’ ἐπὶ τὸ ψυχρότερον ἧττόν τε καὶ μᾶλλον ψυχρόν. οὕτω δ’ ἂν, οἶμαι, καὶ περὶ τῶν φαρμάκων ἀκριβῶς ὀνομάζοντες εὔκρατον ἂν ἐκαλέσαμέν τι καὶ μέσον, ὥσπερ τοὔλαιον· εἰ δέ τι θερμότερον αὐτοῦ, θερμὸν, ὥσπερ τὴν τῆλιν· εἰ δέ τι ψυχρότερον αὐτοῦ, ψυχρὸν, ὥσπερ τὸ ῥόδον. ἀλλὰ τοῦ μὴ χρῆσθαι τοῖς ὀνόμασιν τοῦτον τὸν τρόπον αἴτιον ὑπάρχει τοῦ μὴ ψύχεσθαι πάντα τὰ σώματα πρὸς τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων, οἷον καὶ τὸ ῥόδινόν ἐστι. τά τε γὰρ ὁμοίως αὐτῷ ψυχρὰ καὶ μᾶλλον ψυχρὰ, τὰ μὲν οὐδ’ ὅλως ἀλλοιοῖ, τὰ δὲ καὶ θερμαίνει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τοῦ μὴ χρῆσθαι ... αἴτιον ὑπάρχει τοῦ μὴ ψύχεσθαι
For indeed we sometimes call what is mixed in due proportion 568 "hot", as for instance a bath of that sort and a drink, and also what falls short of this by not much, and in addition to these everything hotter still. But if we were using the names precisely, we would call the well-proportioned not "hot" but "well-tempered"; and whatever is more immoderate than this, that which is immoderate in the direction of the hotter we would call hot to a lesser and to a greater degree, and that which is in the direction of the colder cold to a lesser and to a greater degree. And so too, I think, in the case of drugs, if we were naming precisely, we would have called something well-tempered and intermediate, like olive oil; and if something is hotter than it, hot, like fenugreek; and if something is colder than it, cold, like the rose. But the reason we do not use the names in this way is that not all bodies are cooled by such drugs as rose-oil is. For those bodies that are cold in like degree to it and those that are colder still, in the one case it does not alter at all, and in the other it even heats.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐχρώ μεθα, ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ βαλανεῖον τὸ τοιοῦτον καὶ τὸ πόμα, τοῦ μὴ ψύχεσθαι πάντα τὰ σώματα πρὸς τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων, οἷον καὶ τὸ ῥόδινόν ἐστι
For we sometimes call what is mixed in due proportion 568 'hot': so, for example, we call such a bath hot, and such a drink, and also whatever falls short of it by not much, and, besides these, everything that is hotter still. But if we used names precisely, we would call what is in due proportion not 'hot' but 'well-tempered'. Whatever departs further from due proportion than this, we would call 'hot' to a lesser or greater degree when it is immoderate toward the hotter, and 'cold' to a lesser or greater degree when it is immoderate toward the colder. In the same way, I think, if we named drugs precisely, we would have called a drug 'well-tempered' and 'intermediate', as olive oil is. If a drug were hotter than this, we would have called it 'hot', as fenugreek is. If it were colder than this, we would have called it 'cold', as the rose is. But the reason we do not use names in this way is that not all bodies are cooled by such drugs, of which rose oil too is one. For of the bodies that are as cold as it and those that are colder, it does not alter the former at all, and it even heats the latter.

δόξειεν ἂν οὖν ἄλογον ὑπάρχειν, εἴ τι θερμαίνειν ὁμολογοῦντες εἶτα ψυχρὸν προσαγορεύομεν, 569 ὥσπερ εἰ καὶ τὸ φρεατιαῖον ὕδωρ ἐν χειμῶνι, τὸ ταῖς κατεψυγμέναις χερσὶ χλιαρὸν φαινόμενον, ὀνομάζοι τις ψυχρόν. ἐξ οὖν τούτων τῶν προφάσεων ἡ τῶν ὀνομάτων χρῆσις ταραχθεῖσα καὶ τὴν τῶν πραγμάτων ἐπιταράττει γνῶσιν. ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ περὶ ξηροῦ καὶ ὑγροῦ τῶν ὀνομάτων συγχυθέντων καὶ ἡ τῶν πραγμάτων γνῶσις συνεχύθη. καίτοι σμικρὸν εἶναι δοκοῦν τὸ διελέσθαι καλῶς τὰ σημαινόμενα τῶν ὀνομάτων, τὴν μεγίστην ἔχον εὑρίσκεται δύναμιν εἰς τὴν τῶν πραγμάτων γνῶσιν. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν ὑγροῦ καὶ ξηροῦ μετ’ ὀλίγον ἐροῦμεν, ἐπειδὰν τὸν περὶ τοῦ θερμοῦ καὶ ψυχροῦ συμπερανώμεθα λόγον. οὐδὲ γὰρ οὐδὲ λείπειν ἔοικέ τι πολὺ τῶν ἐν αὐτῷ διορισμῶν, ἅπαξ γε σαφῶς πεφηνότος ὡς οὐδὲν θαυμαστὸν ὑφ’ ἑνὸς φαρμάκου διαφέροντα σώματα τὰ μὲν θερμαίνεσθαι, τὰ δὲ ψύχεσθαι, τὰ δὲ μηδέτερον αὐτῶν πάσχειν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προφάσεων
It would seem, then, to be unreasonable if, while agreeing that something heats, we then call it cold, 569 just as if one were also to call well-water in winter cold, the water which appears lukewarm to chilled hands. From these occasions, then, the use of the names, having been thrown into disorder, disorders in turn the knowledge of the things as well. In the same way too, concerning dry and moist, once the names were confounded, the knowledge of the things was confounded along with them. And yet, although distinguishing properly the things signified by the names seems a small matter, it is found to have the greatest power for the knowledge of the things. But about moist and dry we shall speak a little later, when we have brought to a conclusion the account concerning hot and cold. For indeed not much of the distinctions belonging to it seems still to be lacking, once at least it has clearly been shown that it is nothing to wonder at that, under the action of a single drug, differing bodies are some of them heated, others cooled, and others undergo neither of these.

It might seem, then, to be unreasonable if, while agreeing that something heats, we then call it cold, 569 just as if someone were to call cold the well-water in winter, which appears lukewarm to chilled hands. From these reasons, then, the use of names, having been thrown into confusion, also throws into confusion the knowledge of the things themselves. And yet, although it seems a small matter to distinguish well the meanings of names, it is found to have the greatest force for the knowledge of things. For not much of the distinctions belonging to it seems still to be lacking, now that it has once been clearly shown that it is nothing surprising if, by one drug, different bodies are in some cases heated, in others cooled, and in others undergo neither of these.

Μέσον οὖν τινα νόησόν μοι τῶνδε, τήν τε φύσιν εὐθὺς ἐξ ἀρχῆς εὔκρατον, ἔθεσί τε προσήκουσιν τὴν εὐκρασίαν ταύτην διαφυλάττοντα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For in the relative sense it is very easy, when one comes to the particular cases, to say that rose oil, when applied to such-and-such a body, is of a nature to preserve it in the mixture in which it found it, whereas when brought to another body of such-and-such a kind it heats 570 and another it cools. But to make a universal pronouncement about its power is difficult without first drawing distinctions and reaching an agreement about the names. The distinction and the agreement would be as follows: since the bodies of human beings differ very greatly both in their mixtures from the beginning and in those that accrue to them according to their ages and in those according to the habits of their way of life, it is surely necessary that they be altered in different ways by the same drugs. At any rate, if you compare a young man, hot by nature, a labourer by way of life, with an old woman who is also colder by nature, you would find the excess no smaller than that of one of the drugs that heat considerably in relation to those that cool. Conceive for me, then, someone intermediate between these, both well-mixed in nature right from the beginning and preserving this good mixture by appropriate habits.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐν μὲν γὰρ τῷ πρός τι, τοιῷδε, ὑπεροχὴν
For in the relative sense it is very easy, when one comes to particulars, to say that rose oil, when applied to a body of such a kind, is naturally apt to preserve it in the mixture in which it found it, while when applied to some other body of a certain kind it heats 570, and to another it cools. But to pronounce universally about its power is difficult without first distinguishing the names and agreeing upon them. The distinction and the convention would be of the following kind. Since the bodies of human beings differ very greatly, both in their original mixtures and in those that accrue to them according to their ages and according to the habits of their life, it is surely necessary that they are altered differently by the same drugs. At any rate, if you compare a young man who is hot by nature and a labourer in his way of life with an old woman who is also colder by nature, you would find the excess to be no less than that of one of the strongly heating drugs over the cooling ones. Conceive for me, then, someone midway between these, who is well-mixed in nature right from the start and who preserves this good mixture by suitable habits.

Greek (Kühn)
καὶ τρεῖς τούτους ὥσπερ σκοποὺς κράσεως ἐν τῇ διανοίᾳ παραθέμενος ἐπίσκεψαι τὸ καθόλου θερμὸν, ἢ ψυχρὸν, ἢ ξηρὸν, ἢ ὑγρὸν φάρμακον, ὡς πρὸς ποίαν ἐξ αὐτῶν κρᾶσιν ἀποβλέποντα 571 προσαγορεύειν ἐστὶ βέλτιον. ἆρά γε πρὸς τὴν εὐκρατοτάτην, ἥπερ δὴ καὶ μία τίς ἐστι καὶ μέση τῶν ἄλλων ἁπασῶν, ἢ πρὸς τὰς ἀμέτρους καὶ δυσκράτους καὶ ἀδιορίστους καὶ σχεδὸν ἀπείρους τὸ πλῆθος; ἐμοὶ μὲν γὰρ οὐδὲ παραβλητέον εἶναι δοκεῖ τὸ τῆς διαιρέσεως, οὐδὲ σκέψεώς τινος ἐπιδεὲς, ἀλλ’ ἄντικρυς φαίνεσθαι τῷ καὶ σμικρὸν νοῦν ἔχοντι τὸ κατωρθωμένον σῶμα καὶ ἁπλοῦν καὶ ἓν οἷον κανόνα τινὰ καὶ σκοπὸν ὑποθέμενον ἀπευθύνειν, οὕτω κρῖναι καὶ τἄλλα πρὸς τοῦτο. κείσθω τοίνυν ἡμῖν τὸ τοιοῦτον σῶμα τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως κανών. καὶ τὸ μὲν ὁμοίαν τῇ τούτου κράσει θερμασίαν ἀνάπτον εὔκρατον ὀνομαζέσθω, κᾂν ὅτι μάλιστα θερμαίνειν ἐπιφανῶς δοκῇ πρεσβύτην κατεψυγμένον. τὸ δ’ ἤτοι θερμαῖνον, ἢ ψῦχον τὸ τοιοῦτον σῶμα, καλείσθω τὸ μὲν θερμαῖνον θερμὸν, τὸ δὲ ψῦχον ψυχρόν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παραβλητέον, ἀπευθύνειν
And having set these three before your mind as, so to speak, the targets of temperament, consider the drug that is hot, or cold, or dry, or moist in general, and ask with reference to which temperament among these it is 571 better to look in naming it. Is it to the best-tempered one, which is indeed one single thing and the mean of all the others, or to those that are immoderate and ill-tempered and undefined and almost infinite in number? For to me the question of this division does not even seem to admit of comparison, nor to stand in need of any inquiry; rather it appears plainly, to anyone possessed of even a little sense, that one should take the body that is rightly constituted and simple and one, laying it down as a kind of rule and target, and steer straight by it, and so judge the others too with reference to it. Let such a body, then, be laid down by us as the rule of the power of drugs. And let that drug which kindles a warmth similar to the temperament of this body be named well-tempered, even if it seems ever so manifestly to heat an old man who is chilled. But as for that which either heats or cools such a body, let the one that heats be called hot, and the one that cools cold.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παραβλητέον, ἀπευθύνειν, κατωρθωμένον
And, having set these three side by side in your mind as standards, as it were, of mixture, consider the drug that is hot, or cold, or dry, or moist without qualification: looking to which of these mixtures 571 is it better to give it that name? Is it to the best-tempered, which is indeed one single mixture and the mean of all the others, or to the immoderate, ill-tempered and indeterminate ones, which are almost infinite in number? To me the two members of this division do not even seem comparable, nor does the question seem to need any examination; rather, it appears plainly, to anyone with even a little sense, that one should lay down the rightly constituted body, which is simple and single, as a kind of rule and standard, direct oneself by it, and so judge the other things by reference to it. Let such a body, then, be laid down by us as the rule for the power of drugs. Let what kindles a warmth similar to this body's mixture be called well-tempered, even if it seems very manifestly to warm an old man who has been chilled. But of what either warms or cools such a body, let that which warms be called hot, and that which cools cold.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀποστάσεων, ἐπί τε τῶν ὑγραινόντων φαρμάκων
And let there be made, for the sake of clear teaching, an ordering of the degrees of distance of these very things: on the side of the cold, the first being that in which one would place rose oil, the second that in which the rose itself will be, and a third after this and a fourth, until we arrive in our account at the 572 coldest things, hemlock and poppy-juice and mandrake and henbane. On the side of the hot, in turn, dill and fenugreek first, and next in order as second whatever appear to be second, and then third and fourth, until we arrive at those which burn. And in the same manner, in the case of the moistening drugs too, beginning again from the well-proportioned [mean], let us make orders in succession as far as the extremes. For the use of such knowledge for the therapeutic method is no small one. Or rather, if one must tell the truth, if someone does not draw the distinctions in this way, he will harm rather than benefit those who read him. As it is now, in addition to the other errors which the majority of physicians commit when expounding the powers of the simple drugs, one can also find this, which is the more harmful: among the heating drugs, for instance, there may be written dill and fenugreek and misy and lime, and among the cooling ones rose oil and mandrake and hemlock, as though fenugreek were close to lime, or rose oil to hemlock, and not separated by the many orders of drugs lying between them.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀποστάσεων, ἐξ ἧς, ἐπί τε τῶν ὑγραινόντων, ὑπάρχουσαν
For the sake of clear instruction, let there be established a series of ranks of these deviations themselves. Towards the cold, the first, in which one might place rose oil; the second, in which the rose itself will be; and a third after this one, and a fourth, until in our account we arrive at the 572 coldest things: hemlock, poppy juice, mandrake and henbane. Towards the hot, in turn, dill and fenugreek first; second, next in order, whatever things appear to be second; and so third and fourth, until we arrive at the things that burn. In the same way also with the moistening drugs, starting again from the moderate, let us make ranks in sequence up to the extremes. For the use of such knowledge for the therapeutic method is not small. Or rather, if one must speak the truth, unless one makes distinctions in this way, one will harm rather than benefit one's readers. For as things now stand, in addition to the other errors that most physicians commit when they expound the powers of simple drugs, one can also find this one, the more harmful: among the heating drugs there is written, as it may happen, dill and fenugreek and misy and lime, and among the cooling drugs rose oil and mandrake and hemlock, as though fenugreek were close to lime, or rose oil to hemlock, and they were not separated by many intermediate ranks of drugs.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χυλῶν
But 573 we, at any rate, shall try, so far as we are able, to bring so great and difficult a task into some order and to write down both those drugs that are near one another in their powers and those that are far apart. And let us do this not by entrusting the judgment of them to plausible arguments, but to determinate experience, just as this too has often been said in what precedes. It is surely evident that such a judgment is safe, but at the same time long and involving much labour for us who are going to arrive through it at the end of what is proposed; yet for those who wish to read through these writings it alone is capable of making them skilled in the art and of providing, as it were, a certain eye that discerns the truth. Accordingly, having added in this book whatever is still lacking for the whole elementary exposition, we shall speak in the fourth about the power of the flavours (χυλοί), since this too seemed good to our companions; and in the fifth about the secondary and composite powers in all simple drugs in general; and then in this way we shall pass over to the particulars in the subsequent treatises.

But 573 we at least shall try, as far as we are able, to bring so great and difficult a task into some kind of order, and to write down both the things that are near to one another in their powers and those that are far apart. Greek (Kühn)
574 Λείπει δὴ πρῶτον μὲν, οὗ πολλάκις ἤδη καὶ πρόσθεν ὁ λόγος ἠναγκάσθη μνημονεῦσαι, τελέως δ’ οὐκ ἐπεξῆλθεν. ἔστι δὴ τοῦτο σχεδὸν ἅπαντα τὰ φάρμακα, κᾂν ἁπλᾶ πρὸς αἴσθησιν φαίνηται, τῇ φύσει γοῦν ὑπάρχει σύνθετα, καὶ πολλάκις γε καὶ τὰς ἐναντιωτάτας ἐν αὐτοῖς ἔχει δυνάμεις, οἷον ἐκκριτικήν τε καὶ σταλτικήν παχυντικήν τε καὶ λεπτυντικὴν, ἀραιωτικήν τε καὶ πυκνωτικὴν, ἐμπλαστικήν τε καὶ διαῤῥυπτικὴν, συντατικήν τε καὶ χαλαστικὴν, ἁπάσας τε τὰς ἄλλας ἀντιθέσεις, ὡς ἐν τῷ μετὰ ταῦτα λόγῳ δείξομεν. οὐδὲν δὲ θαυμαστὸν, εἰ ἐπὶ τῶν τοιούτων δυνάμεων ἑνὶ καὶ ταὐτῷ φαρμάκῳ θερμαντική τε καὶ ψυκτικὴ φαίνοιτο, ἢ ξηραντική τε καὶ ὑγραντικὴ, λεπτομερής τε καὶ παχυμερής. εἰ μὴ γὰρ αἱ πρῶται καὶ δραστικώταται τὴν τοιαύτην εἶχον συμπλοκὴν, οὐδ’ ἂν αἱ μετὰ ταύτας ὁμοίως συνεπλάκησαν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
574 There remains, then, first, a point which the discussion has often already been compelled to mention even before, but did not go through completely. This is that nearly all drugs, even if they appear simple to sense-perception, are at any rate compound in their nature, and often indeed have within themselves even the most opposite powers, such as an excretory and a checking one, a thickening and a thinning one, a rarefying and a condensing one, a clogging and a cleansing one, a tensing and a relaxing one, and all the other oppositions, as we shall show in the discussion after this. And it is nothing remarkable if, in the case of such powers, in one and the same drug a heating and a cooling power should appear, or a drying and a moistening one, a fine-particled and a coarse-particled one. For if the first and most active powers did not have such a combination, neither would those that come after them have been similarly combined.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συντατικήν, ἐκκριτικήν, διαῤῥυπτικὴν, ἐπὶ τῶν τοιούτων δυνάμεων, συμπλοκὴν
574 What remains, then, is first the point which the account has often been compelled to mention before, but has not gone through completely. It is this: nearly all drugs, even if they appear simple to perception, are at any rate composite in their nature, and often indeed they contain within themselves the most opposite powers, such as an expelling and a checking power, a thickening and a thinning, a rarefying and a condensing, a clogging and a cleansing, a tightening and a relaxing, and all the other oppositions, as we shall show in the account after this. And it is no wonder if, in the case of such powers, one and the same drug should appear both heating and cooling, or drying and moistening, fine-particled and coarse-particled. For if the first and most active powers did not have such an interweaving, neither would those that come after them have been interwoven in the same way.

K11.574-575K. XI 574–575, ch. 3.14, 3.15 difficulty mid (2.55/100; thin 3, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ἀλλ’ ἡμεῖς ἐὰν αὐτοὶ μίξωμεν, εἰ οὕτως ἔτυχεν, ῥοῦ χυλὸν μέλιτι, τὸ μικτὸν ἐξ ἀμφοῖν συγχωροῦμεν οὐ στυπτικὴν μόνην, ἀλλὰ καὶ ῥυπτικὴν ἔχειν δύναμιν οὐδ’ ἐφεκτικὴν ﻿ 575 γαστρὸς μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ κενωτικήν. ἐν δὲ τῷ γάλακτι τὰς δύο περιέχεσθαι δυνάμεις, ἐκκριτικήν τε ἅμα καὶ σταλτικὴν γαστρὸς, οὐκ ἔθ’ ὁμοίως συγχωροῦμεν. καίτοι τοῦτο μὲν καὶ διακρίνεται ῥᾳδίως εἰς ὀῤῥὸν καὶ τυρὸν, καὶ πᾶσιν ὁμολογεῖται τὸ μὲν ὑπάγειν τὴν γαστέρα, τὸ δὲ ἐπέχειν. Τοῦτ’ οὐκ ἔτι συγχωροῦμεν ἑτοίμως καὶ ἐν τῇ κράμβῃ. ἐκθλίβοντες μὲν γὰρ αὐτῆς τὸν χυλὸν καὶ μόνον, ἢ μετὰ μέλιτος καταῤῥοφοῦντες ὁρῶμεν καθαρτικὸν, οὐ μὴν ἐσθίειν γε δυνάμενοι τὸ λοιπὸν, ἀπιστοῦμεν ὅτι σταλτικόν ἐστιν. ἀλλ’ ἄν τις ὅλην ἑψήσας αὐτὴν ἀποχέῃ τὸ πρότερον ὕδωρ, ἐν ᾧ τὸν χυλὸν ἐναπέθετο, κᾄπειτα αὖθις ἐμβαλὼν ἑτέρῳ καθαρῷ, καὶ πάλιν ἑψήσας ἀποχέῃ καὶ τοῦτο, τὸν μὲν χυλὸν εὑρήσει καθαίροντα, τὴν δὲ κράμβην αὐτὴν ἐπέχουσαν τὴν γαστέρα καὶ πολὺ μᾶλλον εἰ μὴ δὶς, ἀλλὰ τρὶς ἢ τετράκις ἀποχέῃ τις τὸ ὕδωρ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But if we ourselves mix, as it may happen, juice of sumach with honey, we concede that what is mixed from both has not only an astringent but also a cleansing power, and not only a power retentive of the belly 575 but also an evacuating one. But that in milk the two powers are contained, one excretory and at the same time one that checks the belly, we no longer concede in the same way. And yet this is even easily separated into whey and curd, and it is agreed by all that the one loosens the belly, the other restrains it. This we still do not readily concede in the case of cabbage too. For when we press out its juice and gulp it down either alone or with honey, we see that it is purgative, but, not being able to eat the remainder, we disbelieve that it is checking. But if someone, having boiled the whole of it, pours off the first water, in which it deposited its juice, and then, having thrown it again into other clean water and boiled it again, pours off this too, he will find the juice purging, but the cabbage itself restraining the belly—and much more so if one pours off the water not twice but three or four times.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σταλτικὴν, ἐναπέθετο, οὐ μὴν ἐσθίειν γε δυνάμενοι τὸ λοιπὸν
But if we ourselves mix, as it may happen, pomegranate juice with honey, we grant that what is mixed from both has not only an astringent but also a cleansing power, and is not only retentive 575 of the belly but also evacuative. That in milk, however, the two powers are contained, the excretory and at the same time the restraining power of the belly, we no longer likewise grant. And yet milk is both easily separated into whey and cheese, and it is agreed by all that the one moves the belly downward, while the other holds it back. This, again, we no longer readily grant in the case of cabbage either. For when we squeeze out its juice and gulp it down, either by itself or with honey, we see that it is purgative; but since we are not able to eat the remainder, we disbelieve that it is restraining. But if someone, having boiled the cabbage whole, pours off the first water, into which it has deposited its juice, and then puts it again into other, clean water, and having boiled it once more pours this off too, he will find that the juice purges, while the cabbage itself holds back the belly; and much more so if one pours off the water not twice but three or four times.

ὅσῳ γὰρ ἂν ἀκριβέστερον ἐναπόθηται τὸν χυλὸν αὐτῷ, καθαρτικώτερον μὲν ἐκεῖνο ποιήσει, τὴν δὲ ὑπόλοιπον αὐτῆς οὐσίαν τὴν οἷον σάρκα τοῦ λαχάνου σταλτικὴν 576 ἐπιδείξεται. οὕτω δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν τεύτλων ἔχει καὶ σχεδὸν ἁπάντων τῶν ἄλλων, ὧν ἤτοι δριμὺς, ἢ νιτρώδης ἢ ἁλυκὸς ὁ χυλός ἐστιν, ἐπεί τοι καὶ τοῖς φακοῖς, καίτοι στύψιν ἱκανὴν ἔχουσι κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν, ὅμως ὑπάρχει τι τοιοῦτον. ἔστι γὰρ καὶ τὸ τούτων ἀφέψημα λαπακτικὸν τῆς γαστρὸς, ἀλλ’ οὗτος μὲν ὁ χυλὸς ἐπίμικτος σαφῶς ἐστιν κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν, ὡς εἰ καὶ νιτρῶδές τι μίξεις αὐστηρῷ. ὁ δὲ τῶν θαλαττίων κογχαρίων, ἁπάντων τε τῶν ἄλλων ὀστρέων χυμὸς, ἁπλῶς ἁλυκός ἐστι καὶ λαπακτικός. αὐτὴ μέντοι γε καὶ τούτων ἡ σὰρξ ἐπέχει γαστέρα, καὶ πειραθήσῃ δὲ ἀληθοῦς τοῦ λεγομένου, σκευάσας ὁμοίως ταῖς κράμβαις αὐτά. καὶ γάρ τοι καὶ ὁ τῶν πρεσβυτέρων ἀλεκτρυόνων ζωμὸς ὑπάγει τὴν γαστέρα, καίτοι τῆς σαρκὸς σταλτικῆς οὔσης. ἔστι δὲ κᾀν τῇ λεπίδι τοῦ χαλκοῦ δύναμις διττὴ, καθάπερ καὶ ποιότης, καὶ γὰρ στύφει καὶ δάκνει· καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τῷ μὲν στύφειν ἐπουλεῖ τὰς ξηρὰς τῶν ἡλκωμένων σαρκῶν, τῷ δ’ αὖ δάκνειν τὰς πλαδαρὰς συντήκει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κογχαρίων, ὀστρέων
For the more thoroughly it deposits its juice in it, the more purgative it will make that [water], while it will show the remaining substance of the cabbage, the flesh as it were of the vegetable, to be checking 576. And so it is also with beets and with nearly all the others whose juice is either pungent or nitrous or salty; since indeed even in lentils, although they have considerable astringency to the taste, there is nevertheless something of this kind. For the decoction of these too is laxative of the belly; but this juice is clearly mixed to the taste, as if you were also to mix something nitrous with a harsh one. But the juice of sea-shellfish and of all the other oysters is simply salty and laxative. Yet the flesh itself of these too holds back the belly, and you will find what is said to be true if you prepare them in the same way as the cabbages. For indeed the broth of older cocks too loosens the belly, although the flesh is checking. And in the scale of copper too there is a twofold power, just as also a twofold quality; for it is both astringent and biting; and on this account, by its being astringent it cicatrizes the dry ones among ulcerated fleshes, while by its biting it melts down the flabby ones.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But if it should be taken into the body, 577 it purges, and does not check the belly. For the astringent power is overcome by the pungent and purgative one, just as if you were also to mix juice of sumach with scammony. For what results from both will be purgative, not checking of the belly, even though to the taste the harsh juice will rather predominate. So too not a few of the purgatives, when given with the flesh of a quince-apple, escape notice in taste and are manifest only in their activity. Some are also given by means of the flesh of the date, others by means of myrtle-berries or with Chian mastic, or with some other of the things that are good for the stomach and astringent, so as both to escape the taste and to upset the stomach less. But even if the astringent things prevail over the purgatives in the quality relative to taste, in the other power at least they are overcome. For often the tongue is overcome in accordance with the preponderating part of the substance, whereas the activity comes about in accordance with the strength of the power.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
When, therefore, a small substance possessing a vehement power, having been mixed with another substance, large in quantity and possessing a weak power, arrives inside the body, it escapes the taste, but accomplishes its work 578 more effectively than that other. These things too are wondered at by the many, and they consider it a puzzle if some astringent body is by nature such as to purge, as for example aloe and scale of copper and burnt copper; for, they say, these ought on the contrary rather to check the belly, not to stimulate it. But that the apple, if it so happens, which together with the scammony is astringent, purges — this at any rate no one wonders at; and yet, setting out from this point, it would have been very easy, I think, to infer in the case of aloe as well that a certain mixed power exists. And if it were possible to separate the purging part in it from the non-purging, as we do in the case of milk and cabbage and very many others, it would no longer be wondered at. And indeed it is even possible, in a way, to do this. For aloe that has been carefully washed moves the belly altogether weakly or not at all. This same thing happens also to scale of copper and to burnt copper itself, as we too have found by trial after washing them thoroughly, their purging being faint. But in any case, the drug-like character of the quality, too, immediately upon tasting, displays the mixed nature of the power.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκείνης, εἰ οὕτως ἔτυχεν, ἄπορον
When, therefore, a small substance having a vehement power, mixed with another substance that is abundant and has a weak power, arrives inside the body, it escapes the notice of the taste, but it accomplishes its work 578 more effectively than that other substance. These things too are marvelled at by the many, and they consider it a matter without explanation if some astringent body is naturally such as to purge, for example aloe, flake of copper and burnt copper; for, they say, these ought on the contrary rather to check the belly, not to urge it on. And indeed it is in some way possible to do this. For aloe that has been carefully washed moves the belly altogether weakly, or not at all. This same thing happens also to flake of copper and to burnt copper itself, as we too have found by trial, having washed them thoroughly: they purge only faintly. But in any case the drug-like character of the quality too, immediately upon tasting, shows the mixed nature of the power.

Greek (Kühn)
579 εἴπερ γὰρ ἦν ἀεὶ μία ποιότης ἐν ἑκάστῳ τῶν τὴν γεῦσιν στυφόντων φαρμάκων, οὐκ ἂν ἦν ποτε αὐτῶν τὰ μὲν ἐδώδιμα, τὰ δὲ φαρμακώδη, ἀλλὰ μόνον ἐν τῷ μᾶλλον ἢ ἧττον στύφειν ἀλλήλων διέφερον. νυνὶ δὲ οὐχ οὕτως ἔχει. φαίνεται γὰρ ἡ μέν τις ἡδεῖα καὶ προσηνὴς στύψις, ἡ δέ τις ἀηδής τε καὶ φαρμακώδης, ἀπίου μὲν καὶ μήλου καὶ ῥοιᾶς ἡδεῖα, λεπίδος δὲ χαλκοῦ καὶ ἀλόης ἀηδής τε καὶ φαρμακώδης. καίτοι γε ἧττον στύφει κυδωνίου μήλου λεπὶς χαλκοῦ, ὥστε μηδὲ τῷ σφοδρῷ τῆς στύψεως ἔχειν ἂν ἐνεγκεῖν τὸ τῆς ποιότητος ἀλλόκοτον. ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ ἄλλα τῶν ἐδωδίμων ἀλόης καὶ λεπίδος χαλκοῦ καὶ αὐτοῦ τοῦ κεκαυμένου χαλκοῦ στύφει μᾶλλον. ἐπὶ μὲν δὴ τῶν τοιούτων ἄντικρυς φαίνεται τὸ μικτὸν τῆς οὐσίας, ἐπ’ ἄλλων δὲ, κᾂν μὴ φαίνηται, συλλογίζεσθαι χρὴ καὶ μὴ νομίζειν ἄλλο μέν τι κατὰ τὴν ἀλόην ἐργάζεσθαι τὴν στύψιν, ἄλλο δέ τι κατὰ τὸ μῆλον. ἓν γὰρ ἐν ἅπασι ποιεῖν εὔλογόν ἐστι κατά γε τὴν ἑαυτοῦ φύσιν, οὐ τήν στύψιν μόνην, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ποιοτήτων ἑκάστην.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔχειν ἂν ἐνεγκεῖν
579 For if there were always a single quality in each of the drugs that are astringent to the taste, it would never be the case that some of them are edible and others drug-like; rather, they would differ from one another only in being more or less astringent. But as things are, this is not so. For one kind of astringency appears pleasant and agreeable, another unpleasant and drug-like: that of pear, apple and pomegranate pleasant, that of scale of copper and aloe unpleasant and drug-like. And yet scale of copper is less astringent than a quince-apple, so that one could not even refer the strangeness of its quality to the vehemence of its astringency. Similarly, other edible things too are more astringent than aloe, scale of copper and burnt copper itself. In such cases, then, the mixed character of the substance is manifestly apparent; in others, even if it is not apparent, one must infer it, and not suppose that astringency effects one thing in the case of aloe and something else in the case of the apple. For it is reasonable that, in all cases, not only astringency but also each of the other qualities does one and the same thing, at least according to its own nature.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐνεγκεῖν
579 For if there were always a single quality in each of the drugs that are astringent to the taste, it would never be the case that some of them are edible and others drug-like; rather, they would differ from one another only in being more or less astringent. But as it is, this is not so. For one astringency appears pleasant and mild, another unpleasant and drug-like: that of pear, apple and pomegranate is pleasant, while that of copper scale and aloe is unpleasant and drug-like. And yet copper scale is less astringent than quince, so that one could not attribute the strangeness of its quality to the intensity of its astringency either. Similarly, other edible things too are more astringent than aloe, copper scale, and burnt copper itself. In such cases, then, the mixed nature of the substance is plainly apparent; in others, even if it is not apparent, one must infer it by reasoning, and not suppose that one thing produces the astringency in aloe and another in the apple. For it is reasonable that a single thing produces, in all cases and according to its own nature, not astringency alone but each of the other qualities as well.

Greek (Kühn)
τῷ δὲ μηδὲν εὑρίσκεσθαι καθαρὸν ἀκριβῶς μηδὲ ἄμικτον ﻿ 580 ἑτέρων ποιοτήτων, αἱ διαφοραὶ τῶν ἐνεργειῶν ἄλλαι κατ’ ἄλλο τῶν στυφόντων εἰσίν. ἄλλο μὲν γὰρ μεσπίλου τοὖργόν ἐστιν, ἄλλο δὲ λεπίδος, ἄλλο δὲ ἀλόης, ἕτερον δὲ μόρων, εἰ οὕτως ἔτυχεν ἢ κικίδος, ἢ σιδίων, ἢ στυπτηρίας, ἢ ῥοῦ, καὶ βέλτιον ἦν, ὅσοι τι περὶ τῶν στυφόντων φαρμάκων τῆς ἐνεργείας ἐτόλμησαν ἀποφήνασθαι, καθόλου περὶ τῆς στύψεως αὐτῆς μᾶλλον ἢ περὶ τῶν στυφόντων εἰρῆσθαι. τὸ μὲν γὰρ στῦφον οὐ στύφει μόνον, ἀλλ’ ἤτοι δριμὺ πρὸς τὸ στύφειν ἐστὶν, ἢ λιπαρὸν, ἢ γλυκὺ, ἢ πικρὸν, ἢ ἁλυκὸν, ἢ ὀξῶδες. ἡ δὲ στύψις ἓν τοῦτο μόνον ἐστὶ διὰ παντὸς ὅπερ ἐστὶ, ὥσπερ καὶ ἡ γλυκύτης καὶ ἡ ἁλυκότης ἑκάστη τε τῶν ἄλλων ποιοτήτων. εἰ δ’ ὥσπερ ἡ στύψις μία, καὶ τὸ στῦφον ἓν ὑπῆρχεν, ἓν ἂν ἦν αὐτοῦ διὰ παντὸς τοὖργον. ἐπεὶ δὲ πρὸς τὸ στύφειν ἔτι καὶ ἄλλας δέδεικται ποιότητας οὐκ ὀλίγας, ἀεὶ μὲν ἐνεργήσει κατὰ πάσας, ἀλλ’ ἐπιφανῶς ἄλλοτε κατ’ ἄλλην τὴν ἐπικρατοῦσαν, ὡς πρὸς τὴν τοῦ πλησιάζοντος σώματος διάθεσιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀξῶδες, ῥοῦ
But because nothing is found that is exactly pure or unmixed 580 with other qualities, the differences of the activities are different in one astringent thing and in another. For the action of the medlar is one thing, that of scale of copper another, that of aloe another, and different again that of mulberries, if it should so happen, or of oak-gall, or of pomegranate rind, or of alum, or of sumach; and it would have been better for those who ventured to make some pronouncement about the activity of astringent drugs to have spoken in general about astringency itself rather than about the astringent things. For the astringent thing is not only astringent, but, in addition to being astringent, is either pungent, or oily, or sweet, or bitter, or salty, or sour. Astringency, however, is this one thing only, throughout, exactly what it is, just as sweetness and saltiness and each of the other qualities also are. And if, just as astringency is one, the astringent thing too were one, its action would throughout be one. But since, in addition to being astringent, it has been shown to possess yet other qualities, not a few, it will always act in accordance with all of them, but conspicuously now according to one, now according to another, namely the one that prevails, in relation to the condition of the body that comes into contact with it.

But because nothing is found that is precisely pure and unmixed 580 with other qualities, the differences of the activities are different in each of the astringent [drugs]. For the effect of medlar is one thing, that of the flake [sc. of copper] another, that of aloe another, and that of mulberries different again, or, as it may happen, of oak-gall, or of pomegranate rinds, or of alum, or of sumac. And it would have been better if those who ventured to make any pronouncement about the activity of astringent drugs had spoken in general about astringency itself rather than about the astringent [drugs]. For the astringent [drug] is not only astringent: in addition to being astringent it is either pungent, or oily, or sweet, or bitter, or salty, or sour. Astringency, on the other hand, is always this one thing only, that which it is, just as sweetness and saltiness and each of the other qualities are. If, just as astringency is one, the astringent [drug] were also one, its effect would always be one. But since, in addition to being astringent, it has been shown [to possess] not a few other qualities as well, it will always act according to all of them, but manifestly now according to one, now according to another, whichever prevails, relative to the condition of the body it comes into contact with.

Greek (Kühn)
φαίνεται γοῦν καὶ τὸ γάλα καὶ ἡ φακὴ καὶ ἡ κράμβη 581 τῶν μὲν ὑπάγοντα, τῶν δὲ ἴσχοντα τὴν γαστέρα. τὴν μὲν οὖν αἰτίαν τοῦ μὴ διὰ παντὸς ἐνεργεῖν ἐπιφανῶς καὶ καθ’ ἕκαστον τῶν σωμάτων μίαν ἡντιναοῦν ποιότητα καὶ δύναμιν τοῦ φαρμάκου κατὰ τὸν ἑξῆς ἐρῶ λόγον. νυνὶ δὲ ἀναμνῆσαι τοῦ γινομένου πρόκειταί μοι μόνον, ἵνα μή τις ἀπιστήσῃ κατὰ τὰ πλεῖστα τῶν φαρμάκων ἐναντίας εἶναι ποιότητάς τε καὶ δυνάμεις. ἀλλὰ καὶ αὐτὸ τοῦτο πολλῆς ἐπισκέψεως ἔχεται καὶ διορισμῶν οὐκ ὀλίγων, εἰ καὶ πᾶσιν ἔδοξε ῥᾴδιον εἶναι τὰς ἐναντίας ἀλλήλοις ποιότητάς τε καὶ δυνάμεις ἐξευρεῖν. ἐξ ἑτοίμου γοῦν ἅπαντες σχεδὸν ἐναντίον εἶναι τίθενται τὸ στύφειν τῷ καθαίρειν. ἔστι δὲ οὐδαμῶς ἐναντίον, ὡς ἐγὼ προϊόντος ἐπιδείξω τοῦ λόγου. κατὰ μέντοι τὸ παρὸν ἐκ περιουσίας ὁ λόγος ἐπεράνθη μοι δεικνύντι, κᾂν ἐναντία τις ὑπάρχειν αὐτὰ τίθεται, μηδὲν συμβαίνειν ἄτοπον, ἐνδεχομένου γε ὄντος εἰς μίαν οὐσίαν ἐνίοτε ἐναντιωτάτας συνέρχεσθαι ποιότητάς τε καὶ δυνάμεις.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
At any rate milk, lentil and cabbage 581 plainly loosen the belly in some people and hold it back in others. Now the cause why any one quality and power whatsoever of a drug does not always act manifestly, and in each individual body, I shall state in the following book. For the present my purpose is only to recall what happens, so that no one may disbelieve that in most drugs there are opposite qualities and powers. But even this very point involves much examination and not a few distinctions, even though it has seemed to everyone an easy matter to discover the qualities and powers that are opposite to one another. At any rate nearly all readily lay it down that being astringent is opposite to purging. But it is in no way opposite, as I shall demonstrate as the discourse proceeds. For the present, however, my argument has been brought to its conclusion by way of surplus, in showing that even if someone posits that these are opposite, nothing absurd follows, since it is indeed possible for the most opposite qualities and powers sometimes to come together in a single substance.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκ περιουσίας, κᾂν ... τίθεται, κατὰ τὸν ἑξῆς ... λόγον
At any rate, milk, lentils and cabbage are seen to loosen the bowel in some people and to bind it in others. 581 The cause why any one quality and power of the drug, whichever it may be, does not act manifestly always and in each of the bodies, I shall state in the following book. For now my only aim is to recall what happens, so that no one may disbelieve that in most drugs there are opposite qualities and powers. Yet this very point also calls for much investigation and not a few distinctions, even if it has seemed easy to everyone to find out the qualities and powers that are opposite to one another. At any rate, nearly everyone readily posits that being astringent is opposite to cleansing. But it is in no way opposite, as I shall demonstrate as the discussion proceeds. For the present, however, my argument has been brought to completion more than sufficiently, since I have shown that even if someone posits them to be opposites, nothing absurd follows, it being possible for the most opposite qualities and powers sometimes to come together into a single substance.

ἐπὶ μέν γε τῶν χυλῶν ἐλέχθη καὶ πρόσθεν 582 ἕτερον μὲν εἶναι τὸ βαρύτατον ἐν αὐτοῖς, ἕτερον δὲ τὸ κουφότατον, ἄλλο δὲ τρίτον ἀναμεμιγμένον τῷ χυλῷ ὅλῳ. καὶ θαυμαστὸν οὐδὲν, εἰ κᾀν τῷ τοῦ ῥόδου χυλῷ τὸ μέν ἐστι βαρύτατον ἀνάλογον ἀμόργῃ καὶ τρυγὶ, τὸ δὲ κουφότατον ἀνάλογον καὶ τοῦτο τοῖς ἐποχουμένοις ἄνθεσιν ἑκάστῳ τῶν χυλῶν· ἄλλο δὲ τρίτον, οἷον ἐν τῷ γλεύκει τὸ ζέον, ἐφ’ οἷς τέταρτον αὐτὸ τὸ εἰλικρινέστατον καὶ καθαρώτατον, οὗ ταῦτά ἐστι τὰ τρία περιττώματα. συμβαίνει δὲ τοὺς μὲν ἄλλους ἅπαντας χυλοὺς ὀξύνεσθαί τε καὶ σήπεσθαι διαφθειρομένους, μόνα δὲ τοὔλαιον καὶ τὸ μέλι καὶ τὸν οἶνον ἐξαρκεῖν εἰς μακρόν. ὥστε οὐδὲ τῇ ζέσει καὶ διακρίσει τῶν ἄλλων χυλῶν ἐναργῶς ἐστιν ἐξευρεῖν ἑκάστου τῶν περιττωμάτων τὴν ἰδέαν, ἀλλ’ εὐθὺς ἐξ ἀρχῆς ἑψήσεώς τέ τινος αὐτοῖς δεῖ καὶ μίξεως τῆς πρὸς ἕτερόν τινα τῶν διαμενόντων χυλῶν. οὕτως οὖν καὶ τὸν τοῦ ῥόδου χυλὸν ἀποτιθέμεθα μέλιτι μιγνύντες, ἢ ἐλαίῳ συνέψοντες, ἢ ἐναποβρέχοντες ὅλα τὰ ῥόδα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ζέσει, ἐποχουμένοις ἄνθεσιν
In the case of juices, at any rate, it was said also earlier 582 that the heaviest part in them is one thing, the lightest part another, and a third something else, mixed through the whole juice. And it is no wonder if in the juice of the rose too there is one part that is heaviest, analogous to the olive-lees (amorge) and the lees of wine, another that is lightest, this too analogous to the "flowers" that float upon each of the juices; and a third, like the part that ferments in must; and in addition to these a fourth, the most unmixed and purest part itself, of which these three are the residues. But it happens that all the other juices turn sour and putrefy as they undergo corruption, and only oil, honey and wine last for a long time. Consequently it is not possible, by means of the fermentation and separation of the other juices, to discover clearly the form of each of the residues; rather, right from the beginning they require some boiling and mixture with some other one of the juices that persist. So then we also store the juice of the rose by mixing it with honey, or boiling it together with oil, or steeping whole roses in it.

In the case of juices, at any rate, it was said earlier too 582 that the heaviest part in them is one thing, the lightest another, and a third something else again, mixed through the whole juice. And it is nothing surprising if in the juice of the rose as well there is a heaviest part, analogous to amurca and lees, and a lightest part, this too analogous to the flowers that ride on the surface of each of the juices; and a third, such as the fermenting part in must; and besides these a fourth, the most unmixed and purest part itself, of which these three are the residues. It happens, however, that all the other juices turn sour and putrefy as they spoil, and that only oil, honey and wine hold out for a long time. So that it is not possible, through the fermentation and separation of the other juices, to discover clearly the form of each of the residues; rather, right from the beginning they require some boiling and mixing with some other one of the juices that keep. Thus, then, we also store the juice of the rose, by mixing it with honey, or by boiling it together with oil, or by steeping the whole roses in them.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑπέθη
But that it is plausible that in this too the four kinds are present — that which 583 is proper to the juice itself and the three residues — is evident to see, and especially because what pertains to their quality is not simple; rather, the rose will seem to you to have some astringency too, if you should wish to chew it thoroughly. It has also a certain sweetness and, as it were, a slight biting. And even if none of these appeared to those tasting it, it would have sufficed, from the fact that it is not completely astringent, to grasp the mixtures of the other juices. For if the harsh one were alone, it would be extreme; so that if it is not extreme, neither is it alone. For whatever genesis of astringency you might posit, if you should conceive that it alone, apart from the others, comes to be in a thing, it is necessary that this be produced at its extreme — just as, if heat alone should come to be in some body, such a thing will be completely fire. For the admixtures of contraries break the extremes.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
In the homoeomerous bodies, then, it is heat and coldness, moistness and dryness that produce all the particular differences; but in the non-homoeomerous bodies there are pungencies and sournesses, harshnesses 584 and acerbities and sweetnesses and oilinesses, so that each of the homoeomerous bodies, in the parts themselves, has not many qualities but one, whereas the whole non-homoeomerous body has very many. For if, say, a thousand homoeomerous bodies are contained in the cyathus of some particular juice, it is possible that a hundred of them are astringent, sixty biting, six hundred are the sweet ones, two hundred the bitter, forty the salty, and that all these are mixed through one another, and that one cannot take any perceptible portion so small that it does not have a share in all of them.

In homoeomerous bodies, then, it is heat and cold, wetness and dryness that produce all the particular differences; in anhomoeomerous bodies it is pungencies and sournesses, harshnesses 584 and acerbities, sweetnesses and oilinesses. ὥστε ἐν τῷ ψαύειν τῆς γλώττης ἐνεργεῖν μὲν ἅπαντα κατὰ τὸ διαφέρον ἕκαστον αὐτῶν μόριον, ᾧπερ ἂν ὁμιλῆσαν τύχῃ, διαδίδοσθαι δὲ τὴν διάθεσιν ἐκ τοῦ πρώτου παθόντος εἰς ὅλην αὐτὴν, ὥσπερ ὅταν εἰς ὕδωρ ψυχρὸν ἕνα δάκτυλον ἀθρόως καθέντες ἐν ψύχει σφοδρῷ φρίξομεν ὅλην τὴν χεῖρα, πολλάκις δὲ καὶ σύμπαν τὸ σῶμα· ταχεῖα γὰρ ἡ διάδοσις ἐν τοῖς αἰσθητικοῖς σώμασιν ἐκ τοῦ πρώτου παθόντος εἰς ὅλον γίνεται τὸ ζῶον, ὥστε καὶ θερμαινομένῳ συνθερμαίνεσθαι καὶ ψυχομένῳ συμψύχεσθαι καὶ ξηραινομένῳ ﻿ 585 συγξηραίνεσθαι καὶ ὑγραινομένῳ συνυγραίνεσθαι, καὶ πολλάκις τἀναντία πάσχειν ὅλον ὑφ’ ἕνα καιρόν. ἵνα δὲ μᾶλλον εἰς ἔννοιαν ἀφίκῃ τοῦ λεγομένου, βέλτιον ἴσως ἐστὶ καὶ παραδείγματι προσέχειν ἑτέρῳ τὸν νοῦν. εἶδες οὖν, οἶμαι, ποτὲ λίθου τινὸς ἐμβληθέντος εἰς ὕδωρ ἀτρεμίζον, ἀρξάμενον μὲν ἐκ τούτου κινεῖσθαι τὸ ὕδωρ, ἐν κύκλῳ δὲ τὴν κίνησιν ἐκτεῖνον, ὥσπερ εἰς κέντρον τὸ πλῆξαν. ὅταν οὖν ὑφ’ ἕνα καιρὸν εἰς διαφέροντας τόπους ἀτρεμίζοντος ὕδατος ἐμβληθέντες δύο λίθοι γεννήσωσι δύο κύκλους δι’ ἀλλήλων φερομένους, ἐναντίας ὄψει κινήσεις ἐν ἅπαντι μορίῳ τοῦ ὕδατος, ὅθεν καὶ ἀπορίαν παρέσχε τὸ φαινόμενον τοῦτο καὶ αὐτοῖς τοῖς φυσικοῖς ἀνδράσιν, εἴθ’ ἕτερα μόρια πρὸς ἑκατέρας τῶν κινήσεων, εἴτε καὶ ταὐτὰ κινεῖται πρὸς ἀμφοτέρων. ὅτι μὲν γὰρ οὐχ ἵστησιν ὁ κύκλος τὸν κύκλον οὔτε κωλύει φέρεσθαι πρόσω προδήλως ὁρᾶται. ταὐτὸν δὴ τούτῳ νόει μοι, ὅταν ὑφ’ ἕνα καιρὸν ἐμβάλλωμεν εἰς μὲν ψυχρὸν ὕδωρ τοὺς πόδας, εἰς δὲ θερμὸν τὰς χεῖρας. εἰς ὅλον μὲν γὰρ τὸ σῶμα τοῦ πάθους ἑκατέρου διέρχεται ἡ κίνησις, ἀπαντῶσι δὲ ἀλλήλαις ὑπεναντίαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὥσπερ εἰς κέντρον τὸ πλῆξαν
so that, in the contact with the tongue, all of them act, each part of them according to its own distinct character, upon whatever part it happens to have come into contact with, and the disposition is transmitted from the part first affected to the whole tongue—just as when, in severe cold, we plunge a single finger all at once into cold water and shiver with the whole hand, and often with the entire body as well. For the transmission in sensitive bodies from the part first affected to the whole animal takes place swiftly, so that when a part is heated the whole is heated along with it, when it is cooled the whole is cooled along with it, when it is dried 585 the whole is dried along with it, and when it is moistened the whole is moistened along with it, and often the whole undergoes contrary affections at one and the same time. But in order that you may come to a better notion of what is being said, it is perhaps better to direct your mind to another example as well. You have seen, I think, at some time, when a stone has been thrown into still water, that the water begins to move from that point and extends the movement in a circle, with the thing that struck it as if at a centre. So when, at one and the same time, two stones thrown into different places in still water generate two circles passing through one another, you will see contrary movements in every part of the water; whence this phenomenon has presented a difficulty even to the natural philosophers themselves, whether it is different parts that move in respect of each of the two movements, or the same parts that are moved by both. For that the one circle does not stop the other, nor prevent it from travelling onward, is plainly seen. Conceive, then, the same thing as this for me, when at one and the same time we plunge our feet into cold water and our hands into hot. For the movement of each of the two affections passes through to the whole body, and they meet one another as contrary movements.

so that, in touching the tongue, all of them act, each of them on whatever different part of it it happens to come into contact with, while the affection is transmitted from the part first affected to the whole tongue; just as, when in severe cold we plunge one finger suddenly into cold water, we shiver over the whole hand, and often over the entire body as well. For in sensitive bodies the transmission from the part first affected to the whole animal takes place quickly, so that the whole is heated together with what is being heated, cooled together with what is being cooled, dried 585 together with what is being dried, and moistened together with what is being moistened, and often the whole suffers contrary things at one and the same time. But in order that you may arrive more fully at a conception of what is being said, it is perhaps better to turn your mind to another example as well. You have seen, I suppose, at some time, when a stone was thrown into still water, the water beginning to move from it and extending its movement in a circle, the thing that struck it being, as it were, the centre. Now when, at one and the same time, two stones thrown into different places of still water generate two circles that travel through one another, you will see contrary movements in every part of the water; whence this phenomenon has also caused perplexity to the natural philosophers themselves, whether different parts are moved for each of the two movements, or the same parts are moved by both. For that the one circle does not stop the other circle, nor prevent it from travelling onward, is plainly seen. Conceive, I ask you, the same thing as this when at one and the same time we plunge the feet into cold water and the hands into hot. For the movement of each of the two affections passes through the whole body, and they meet one another as contraries.

586 διαφέρει δὲ ἴσως οὐδὲν οὐδ’ εἰ μὴ τὴν ἐναντίαν ἴοιεν, ἀλλ’ ἡ μὲν ἀπὸ θατέρου τῶν ποδῶν, ἡ δ’ ἀπὸ θατέρου τὴν ὁρμὴν λαβοῦσα προσέρχοιντο. τηνικαῦτα γὰρ οὔτε μηδὲν πάσχειν ὅλον τὸ σῶμα δυνατὸν εἰπεῖν οὔθ’ ὑπὸ θατέρου μόνον πάσχειν. καὶ γὰρ πάσχει σαφῶς καὶ ὑπ’ ἀμφοῖν πάσχει θερμαινόμενόν τε ἅμα καὶ ψυχόμενον, ὥσπερ, οἶμαι, κᾀν ταῖς λειπυρίαις καὶ τοῖς ἠπιάλοις συμπίπτει πυρετοῖς, πλὴν οὐκ ἔξωθέν γε ἐν ἐκείνοις, ἀλλ’ ἐξ αὐτοῦ τοῦ σώματος ἥ τε τῆς ψύξεως καὶ ἡ τῆς θέρμης ὁρμᾶται διάθεσις. εἰ δὲ μηδὲ νῦν ἕπηταί τις τῷ λεγομένῳ, ἀλλ’ ἐκ τοῦ λεχθησομένου ῥᾷστον αὐτῷ τὴν πεῖραν λαβεῖν. καὶ γὰρ οὐδὲ πόῤῥω τῶν ζητουμένων ἐστὶν, ἀλλ’ ἐξ αὐτῶν τι τῶν τῆς γλώττης ὑπάρχει παθῶν. εἰ γὰρ ἀναμίξας ἀκριβῶς ἶσον ἀψινθίου καὶ μέλιτος ὄγκον ἐπιθείης τῇ γλώττῃ, γλυκύπικρον, ὥσπερ οἱ ποιηταὶ τὸν ἔρωτα προσαγορεύουσι, φανεῖταί σοι τὸ μικτὸν ἐξ ἀμφοῖν, οὐκ ἐν ἄλλῳ μέν τινι χρόνῳ μέλιτος αἰσθανομένης τῆς γλώττης, ἐν ἄλλῳ δὲ ἀψινθίου, οὔτε θατέρου μόνον, ἀλλὰ διὰ παντὸς ἀμφοῖν ἀήθη τινὰ μίξιν μεμιγμένην.

586 And perhaps it makes no difference even if they did not travel in opposite directions, but the one, having taken its impulse from one of the feet, and the other from the other, were to advance. For in that case it is possible neither to say that the whole body is affected by nothing, nor that it is affected by only one of the two. For it is clearly affected, and it is affected by both, being heated and cooled at the same time, just as, I think, happens also in the leipyriai and the epialos fevers, except that in those the condition of cooling and that of heat does not set out from outside, but from the body itself. But if even now someone does not follow what is being said, still from what is about to be said it is very easy for him to obtain the test. For indeed it is not even far from the matters under investigation, but is itself one of the affections of the tongue. For if, having thoroughly mixed an equal bulk of wormwood and of honey, you apply it to the tongue, the mixture of both will appear to you sweet-bitter, as the poets call love, the tongue not perceiving honey at one time and wormwood at another, nor only one of the two, but throughout perceiving both, mixed in a certain unaccustomed mixture.

τοιόνδε 587 τι γίγνεται κᾀν τῷ συνελθεῖν εἰς ἕνα χυμὸν αὐστηρὸν καὶ νιτρώδη δύναμιν, ἤτοι τῆς φύσεως αὐτῆς ἀναμιξάσης ἤ τινος ἰατροῦ συνθέντος. ἥ τε γὰρ γλῶττα τῆς ἑκατέρου τῶν χυμῶν ποιότητος ὑφ’ ἕνα χρόνον αἰσθάνεται, καὶ εἴπερ αἰσθάνεται, δηλονότι καὶ διατίθεται κατὰ τὰς οἰκείας αὐτῶν δυνάμεις, ἕκαστόν τε τῶν ἄλλων τοῦ σώματος μορίων ἀνάγκη πάσχειν ὡσαύτως, εἰ καὶ μὴ τῶν ποιοτήτων αἰσθάνηται· οὐ γὰρ δὴ τῷ γε πάσχειν ἢ μὴ διαφέρειν τῶν ἄλλων ὑποληπτέον τὴν γλῶτταν, ἀλλὰ τῷ μὴ λανθάνειν αὐτὴν μηδὲν τῶν οἰκείων παθῶν ἀκριβείᾳ δυνάμεως αἰσθητικῆς. συντελεῖ μὲν οὖν εἰς τοῦτο καὶ ἡ μανότης αὐτῆς. ῥᾷον γὰρ εἰς τὸ βάθος οἱ χυλοὶ κατιόντες ἐνεργοῦντές τε περὶ τὰ μαλακὰ τῶν μορίων ἰσχυρὰν μὲν τὴν διάθεσιν, ἐναργεστέραν δὲ καὶ τὴν αἴσθησιν αὐτῆς ἀπεργάζονται. ἀλλ’ ἡ μείζων δύναμις ἐν τῷ τῆς αἰσθήσεως ἐστὶν ἀκριβεῖ μᾶλλον, οὐκ ἐν τῷ τῆς διαθέσεως ἰσχυρῷ, τὸ μὲν γὰρ τῆς διαθέσεως ἰσχυρὸν οὐδὲν ἧττον κᾀν τοῖς ἡλκωμένοις γίγνεται μορίοις, ἀλλὰ τῷ τὴν ἀκρίβειαν τῆς αἰσθήσεως ἀπεῖναι λανθάνουσιν αἱ τῶν χυλῶν ποιότητες.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Something of this kind 587 also comes about when a harsh and a nitrous power come together into a single juice, whether nature herself has mixed them or some physician has compounded them. For the tongue perceives the quality of each of the two juices at one and the same time, and if it perceives them, clearly it is also disposed according to their proper powers; and each of the other parts of the body must necessarily be affected in the same way, even if it does not perceive the qualities. For one must not suppose that the tongue differs from the other parts in being affected or not, but in the fact that none of its proper affections escapes its notice, owing to the precision of its perceptive power. Its looseness of texture also contributes to this; for the juices, descending more easily into the depth and acting upon the soft parts, produce a disposition that is strong and a perception of it that is more evident. But the greater power lies rather in the precision of the perception, not in the strength of the disposition; for the strength of the disposition arises no less in ulcerated parts too, but because precision of perception is absent, the qualities of the juices escape notice.

588 ἔνεστι μέντοι καὶ κατ’ ἐκεῖνα καὶ πρὸ ἐκείνων ἔτι, μήπω τοῦ δέρματος ἡλκωμένου, συλλογίσασθαι τὴν μικτὴν ἐνέργειαν ἐκ τῶν ἐναντίων δυνάμεων, ἅμα τοῖς σώμασιν ἐγγινομένην. εἰ γὰρ ἐπιθείης φλεγμαίνοντι μορίῳ στρύχνου χυλὸν, ὄψει πυκνούμενον αὐτὸ καὶ πιλούμενον καὶ τὰς διαπνοὰς ἐπεχομένας. ὥστ’ εἰ καὶ μέχρι πλείονος οὕτω ποιήσαις, οὐδ’ ἐρυθρὸν ἔτι φυλάξαις τὸ μόριον, ἀλλ’ ἤτοι πελιδνὸν ἢ μέλαν ἐργάσῃ, ὁποῖόν τι κᾀν τοῖς ἐρυσιπέλασι ποιοῦσι συνεχῶς οἱ πλεῖστοι τῶν ἰατρῶν. καὶ γὰρ καὶ ταῦτα μελαίνουσι καὶ σκληρύνουσιν ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ, καταψύχοντες ἀμέτρως. εἰ τοίνυν μὴ στρύχνον, ἀλλ’ ἀνθεμίδα κατὰ τοῦ φλεγμαίνοντος ἐπιθείης μέρους, ὄψει πάντα τἀναντία πάσχον αὐτό. καὶ γὰρ ἀραιὸν καὶ κεχυμένον καὶ μαλακὸν καὶ χαλαρὸν ἔσται καὶ διαπνευθήσεται πολὺ τοῦ κατ’ αὐτὸ περιττοῦ καὶ πελιδνὸν καὶ μέλαν οὐδέποτ’ ἔσται, κᾂν πολλοῖς ἐφεξῆς ἡμέραις χρήσῃ τῷ χαμαιμήλῳ, χεῖν γὰρ τοῦτο πέφυκε καὶ διαφορεῖν, οὐ συνάγειν οὐδὲ πυκνοῦν οὐδ’ εἴργειν τὰς διαπνοάς.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
588 Yet it is possible, both in those parts and even before them, while the skin is not yet ulcerated, to infer by reasoning the mixed activity that arises in bodies from the opposite powers at the same time. For if you apply the juice of strychnos to an inflamed part, you will see it being condensed and compacted and the transpirations being held back; so that, if you were to do this for a longer time, you would no longer even preserve the part red, but you would make it either livid or black, just the sort of thing that most physicians continually do in cases of erysipelas as well; for these too they blacken and harden in the course of time by chilling them immoderately. If, then, you were to apply not strychnos but anthemis upon the inflamed part, you will see it undergo all the opposite effects: for it will be rare and diffused and soft and slack, and much of the superfluity in it will be transpired away, and it will never become livid or black, even if you use the chamomile for many days in succession; for this is of a nature to diffuse and to disperse, not to contract or condense or shut off the transpirations.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατ’ ἐκεῖνα, ἅμα τοῖς σώμασιν ἐγγινομένην, πιλούμενον, χεῖν
588 It is possible, however, both in those cases and even before them, while the skin is not yet ulcerated, to infer the mixed action that arises from contrary powers, coming about at the same time in the bodies. For if you apply the juice of strychnos to an inflamed part, you will see it being condensed and compacted, and its transpirations being checked. So that, if you do this for a longer time, you will no longer even keep the part red, but will make it either livid or black, which is just the sort of thing that most physicians continually do in erysipelases as well. For these too they blacken and harden in the course of time, by cooling them immoderately. If, then, you apply not strychnos but anthemis to the inflamed part, you will see it undergoing everything contrary. For it will be rare and diffused and soft and slack, and much of the superfluity in it will be transpired, and it will never be livid or black, even if you use the chamomile for many days in succession; for this is by nature such as to diffuse and to disperse, not to draw together, nor to condense, nor to shut in the transpirations.

K11.588-589K. XI 588–589, ch. 3.16 difficulty low (0.0/100; thin 0, absent 0) (a) pass (b) n/a (c) n/a unit: insufficient
Greek (Kühn)
ὅτι μὲν οὖν ἐστιν ἐναντίας δυνάμεως τῷ στρύχνῳ τὸ χαμαίμηλον ἐκ τῶνδε 589 δῆλον· ὅτι δ’ εἰ κᾂν μίξαις ἄμφω, σαφῶς τὰς ἑκατέρου θεάσῃ δυνάμεις, ἐγὼ μὲν πειρασθεὶς σοὶ λέγω, βουλοίμην δ’ ἂν καὶ σὲ διὰ τῆς πείρας αὐτῆς εἰς γνῶσιν ἀφικέσθαι τῶν γιγνομένου μᾶλλον ἢ διηγουμένῳ μοι πιστεῦσαι. τοῦτο μὲν οὖν οἶδα ὅτι ποιήσει πᾶς ὃς ἂν ἀληθείας ὑπάρχῃ ἐραστής. χρὴ δὲ ὡς ἤδη πεφηνότα τὸν ἐφεξῆς λόγον ἐμὲ περαίνειν. εἰ γὰρ ἐπιτιθέντων ὁμοῦ στρύχνου τε καὶ ἀνθεμίδος, οὔθ’ οὕτως πυκνὸν καὶ σκληρὸν καὶ περιτεταμένον καὶ πελιδνὸν, ὡς εἰ καὶ στρύχνῳ μόνον ἐχρῆτό τις, οὔθ’ οὕτως ἀραιὸν καὶ μαλακὸν καὶ χαλαρὸν καὶ ἐρυθρὸν, ὡς εἰ καὶ χαμαιμήλῳ μόνον ἐχρῆτό τις, ἀλλὰ τὴν μέσην ἑκατέρων τῶν εἰρημένων διάθεσιν ὁρᾶται λαμβάνον τὸ μόριον, εὔλογόν ἐστιν ἀμφότερα τὰ φάρμακα κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτῶν ἐνεργῆσαι δύναμιν. εἴτε γὰρ μηδέτερον ἔδρασεν, ἀλλ’ ἡσύχασεν ἄμφω, τὴν ἐξ ἀρχῆς ἂν ἐφύλαττε τὸ μέρος διάθεσιν, ὡς εἰ καὶ μηδὲν ὅλως ἐπεθήκαμεν, εἴτε τὸ μὲν ἕτερον αὐτῶν ἐνήργησε, τὸ δὲ ἕτερον ἐνικήθη τελέως, οὐκ ἂν τὴν μέσην διάθεσιν, ἀλλὰ τὴν ἑτέραν τῶν ἄκρων ἐπεπόνθει τὸ μέρος.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῶν γιγνομένου
That chamomile, then, is of a power contrary to that of strychnos is clear from these 589 considerations; and that, if you should also mix the two, you will clearly observe the powers of each, I tell you from having made the trial myself, though I should wish that you too arrive at knowledge of what happens through the trial itself rather than trust me as I describe it. This, indeed, I know that everyone will do who is a lover of truth. But I must bring the ensuing argument to its conclusion as though these things had already been shown. For if, when strychnos and chamomile are applied together, the part is seen to become neither so dense and hard and tightly stretched and livid as if one had used strychnos alone, nor so loose-textured and soft and slack and red as if one had used chamomile alone, but to take on the condition intermediate between the two that have been described, it is reasonable that both drugs have acted in accordance with their own power. For if neither had acted, but both had remained inert, the part would have kept the condition it had from the beginning, as if we had applied nothing at all; and if one of them had acted while the other had been completely overcome, the part would have been affected not with the intermediate condition but with one or the other of the extremes.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: εἰ κᾂν (ὅτι δ’ εἰ κᾂν μίξαις: the combination εἰ κἄν is anomalous; rendered simply as 'if you should mix'), γιγνομένου (τῶν γιγνομένου: presumably for τῶν γιγνομένων, 'what happens'), ἐπιτιθέντων (genitive absolute without expressed subject; taken impersonally as 'when ... are applied'), περιτεταμένον (rendered 'tightly stretched', i.e. tense)
That camomile, then, is of a power contrary to strychnos is 589 clear from these things. That if you should mix both, you will clearly observe the powers of each, I tell you having made trial of it myself; but I would rather that you too should arrive at knowledge of what happens through experience itself than put your trust in me as I relate it. This, I know, everyone will do who is a lover of truth. But I must carry through the argument that follows as though this had already been shown. For if, when strychnos and anthemis are applied together, the part is seen to take on neither so dense and hard and tightly stretched and livid a condition as if one had used strychnos alone, nor so rare and soft and lax and red a condition as if one had used camomile alone, but the condition intermediate between each of those mentioned, it is reasonable that both drugs acted according to their own power. For if neither had acted, but both had remained at rest, the part would have kept its original condition, as if we had applied nothing at all; and if one of them had acted while the other was completely overcome, the part would have suffered not the intermediate condition but one or other of the extremes.

καὶ μὴν εἴπερ ἑκάτερον ﻿ 590 ἐνήργησεν, ἦν δὲ ταῦτα ἐναντία ταῖς δυνάμεσιν, ἔπασχε δὴ τἀναντία κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν χρόνον τὸ σῶμα. οὐ γὰρ δὴ μερισάμενά γε τοὺς χρόνους ἐνήργησεν, ὥστε εἰ τύχοι δι’ ὅλης ἡμέρας ἐπιτεθέντων ἀμφοῖν ἕξ μὲν ταῖς πρώταις ὥραις θάτερον τῶν φαρμάκων, ἓξ δὲ ταῖς ὑπολοίποις θάτερον ἐνεργῆσαι. ληρώδης γὰρ ἡ ὑπόθεσις αὕτη καὶ πάσης οὐκ ἀποδείξεως μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ πιθανότητος ἔρημος. οὔκουν οὐχ οὕτως συνέβη χυθῆναί τε καὶ συναχθῆναι τὴν ὑποκειμένην οὐσίαν, ὡς τοῖς λουσαμένοις μὲν ἐν βαλανείῳ, μετὰ ταῦτα δὲ εἰς ψυχρὸν ὕδωρ ἐμβᾶσιν, ἀλλὰ μᾶλλον ὡς εἰ τῷ λουομένῳ προσραίνοις ὕδατος ψυχροῦ. οὕτω γὰρ οὐκ ἐν διαφέρουσι χρόνοις, ἀλλὰ καθ’ ἕνα τἀναντία πείσεται. ὡς οὖν ἐπὶ τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων οὐδὲν ἐστι θαυμαστὸν ἐναντίας ἅμα διαθέσεις ἐγγίγνεσθαι τοῖς σώμασιν, οὕτως οὐδ’ ἐπὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν, ὅταν καὶ ταῦτα πρὸς μὲν τὴν ἄλλην αἴσθησίν τε καὶ διάγνωσιν ἁπλᾶ φαίνεται, πρὸς δὲ τὴν φύσιν αὐτὴν ὑπάρχῃ σύνθετα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And indeed, if each of them 590 acted, and these were opposite in their powers, then the body was affected in opposite ways at the same time. For surely they did not act by dividing up the times between them, so that, say, when both had been applied for a whole day, one of the drugs acted during the first six hours and the other during the remaining six. For this hypothesis is nonsensical and destitute not only of all demonstration but also of all plausibility. Therefore the underlying substance did not come to be diffused and contracted in the way that happens to those who have washed in a bath and afterwards stepped into cold water, but rather as if you were to sprinkle cold water on someone while he is bathing. For in this way he will undergo the opposite affections not at different times but at one and the same time. As, then, in the case of compound drugs it is nothing to wonder at that opposite dispositions arise simultaneously in bodies, so neither is it in the case of simple ones, when these too appear simple with respect to sensation in general and diagnosis, but are in respect of their very nature compound.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὔκουν οὐχ, χυθῆναί, συναχθῆναι, πρὸς μὲν τὴν ἄλλην αἴσθησίν τε καὶ διάγνωσιν
And indeed, if each 590 acted, and these were contrary in their powers, then the body was undergoing contraries at the same time. For they certainly did not act by dividing the times between them, so that, for instance, when both had been applied for a whole day, one of the drugs acted during the first six hours and the other during the remaining six. For this hypothesis is nonsensical, and devoid not only of all demonstration but even of plausibility. Therefore the underlying substance was not diffused and drawn together in the way it is for those who have washed in a bath and afterwards stepped into cold water, but rather as if you were to sprinkle cold water on someone while he is bathing. For in this way he will undergo the contraries not at different times but at one and the same time. Just as, then, in the case of compound drugs there is nothing surprising in contrary conditions arising in bodies at the same time, so neither is there in the case of simple drugs, when these too appear simple to perception and diagnosis in other respects, but are compound with respect to their nature itself.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And indeed, just as in the cases where we ourselves compound and mix something, sometimes, as it may happen, putting in more of the 591 astringent juice, sometimes the sweet, or bitter, or salty, or pungent, or some other of the opposites, and sometimes both in equal amounts, so it is possible also for nature, in trees and shrubs and herbs and animals and in the earth itself, to mix the opposite juices either in their bulks or in their powers, and sometimes also one of the two as predominant, so that their activities are alike in the equal mixtures, but different in the unequal ones. Let no one, then, as was said before, straightaway suppose that to discourse about an astringent juice and about astringency itself is the same thing. For astringency is conceived as one simple thing, whereas the astringent body is of two kinds, just as the heating body also is: the one simple and extreme, such as fire, the other heating by predominance of heat, such as blood.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χυμοὺς, ἔστι δ’ ὅτε καὶ τὸν ἕτερον ἐπικρατέστερον, ὁμοίας
Moreover, just as with the things that we ourselves compound and mix, putting in, as it may happen, sometimes more of the 591 astringent juice, sometimes more of the sweet, or bitter, or salty, or pungent, or some other of the opposites, and sometimes both in equal amounts, so too it is possible for nature, in trees and shrubs and herbs and animals and in the earth itself, to mix the opposite flavours, either in their bulks or in their powers. And sometimes she also makes the one more predominant, so that their activities too are similar in the equal mixtures but differ in the unequal ones. Let no one, then, as has also been said before, suppose that it is the same thing to discourse about an astringent juice and about astringency itself. For astringency is conceived as one simple thing, whereas the astringent body is twofold, just as the heating body is: the one simple and extreme, such as fire, the other by predominance of heat, such as blood.

K11.591-592K. XI 591–592, ch. 3.16, 3.17 difficulty high (5.73/100; thin 4, absent 5) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 13/14 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
καὶ στῦφον ἤδη τὸ μέν ἐστιν ἄκρον, οἷον εἰ τύχοι στυπτηρία καὶ μελαντηρία καὶ κικὶς καὶ ῥοῦς, τὸ δὲ τῷ μετέχειν τι καὶ τῆς στυφούσης ποιότητος, οἷον ἄπιόν τε καὶ οἶνος καὶ μῆλον 592 καὶ ῥόδον. ὀλίγιστον γὰρ τὸ στῦφον ἐν πολλαπλασίῳ τῷ μὴ τοιούτῳ μέμικται κατὰ τὸ ῥόδον, ὥστε καὶ τῆς στύψεως ἔργα βραχέα κατὰ λόγον ἐστὶν, ἄχρι τοῦ μὴ διαλύεσθαι τῶν ὁμιλούντων αὐτῷ σωμάτων τὸν τόνον, ὑπὸ τῆς ἐν τῷ ῥόδῳ χαλαστικῆς δυνάμεως. οὐδὲν οὖν θαυμαστὸν ἄκος αὐτὸ φλεγμονῶν ὑπάρχειν καὶ μάλιστα ἐν ἐπιδόσει τε καὶ αὐξήσει, μᾶλλον δὲ καὶ ἀρχομένων ἔτι. Τὸ δὲ ῥόδινον, ὡς ἂν ἐλαίου τε καὶ ῥόδου μεταξὺ τὴν φύσιν ὑπάρχον ἐν ταῖς αὐξήσεσι τῶν φλεγμονῶν, ἄριστον εἶναι φάρμακον. αὐτὸ μὲν γὰρ τοὔλαιον ἐν ταῖς ἀκμαῖς τῶν φλεγμονῶν χρηστὸν, ὡς ἥ τε πεῖρα δείκνυσι κᾀν τοῖς τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου γράμμασιν ἐροῦμεν τὴν αἰτίαν. τὸ δὲ ῥόδινον ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ τῶν ἁπλῶν, ἐξ ὧν ἐγένετο κερασθὲν, ἐν τῷ μεταξὺ καιρῷ τὴν ὠφέλειαν ἐπιδείκνυται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μελαντηρία
And so too the astringent: one kind is extreme, as for instance alum and melanteria and gall-nut and sumach, while the other is so by partaking to some degree also of the astringent quality, such as pear and wine and apple 592 and rose. For in the rose a very small amount of what is astringent is mixed with a many-times-greater amount of what is not of that kind, so that the effects of its astringency too are proportionately slight, extending only so far that the tone of the bodies which come into contact with it is not dissolved by the relaxing power in the rose. It is therefore no wonder that it is a remedy for inflammations, and especially those in their increase and growth, or rather also those still beginning. And rose oil, inasmuch as it is in its nature intermediate between oil and rose, is the best drug in the increases of inflammations. For oil itself is useful at the peaks of inflammations, as experience shows, and we shall state the cause in the books of the Therapeutic Method; but rose oil, having been blended into a state intermediate between the simples from which it came to be, displays its benefit at the intermediate time.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μελαντηρία, ἄχρι τοῦ μὴ διαλύεσθαι
And so too with the astringent: one kind is extreme, such as, for instance, alum, melanteria, oak-gall and sumach; the other is astringent by partaking to some degree of the astringent quality, such as pear, wine, apple 592 and rose. For in the rose a very small amount of the astringent is mixed with a many times greater amount of what is not such, so that the effects of its astringency are, reasonably enough, slight. They go only so far as to keep the tone of the bodies in contact with it from being dissolved by the relaxing power in the rose. It is therefore no wonder that it is a remedy for inflammations, especially in their progression and increase, or rather even while they are still beginning. Nor is it any wonder that rose oil, being intermediate in nature between olive oil and rose, is the best drug in the increases of inflammations. For olive oil itself is useful at the peaks of inflammations, as experience shows; and in the books of the Therapeutic Method we shall state the cause. But rose oil is intermediate between the simples from whose blending it came to be, and so it displays its benefit at the intermediate time.

K11.592-593K. XI 592–593, ch. 3.17 difficulty low (1.1/100; thin 0, absent 2) (a) pass (b) pass 6/6 (c) pass 12/12 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
κατὰ μὲν γὰρ τὰς ἀρχὰς ἀποκρούεσθαι χρὴ μᾶλλον καὶ στέλλειν τὰς ἐπιῤῥοὰς, κατὰ δὲ τὰς ἀκμὰς ἀδήκτως διαφορεῖν, κατὰ δὲ τὰς ἐπιδόσεις ἀποκρούεσθαί τε ἅμα καὶ διαφορεῖν, ὃ χωρὶς μὲν τῶν προειρημένων λογισμῶν ἀκούσας τις ἴσως 593 ἀπιστήσειεν. νυνὶ δ’ οὐκ οἶδα εἴ τις οὕτως ἐστὶν ἐνεὸς ὡς μήπω πεπεῖσθαι τῷ λόγῳ. καὶ γὰρ τὰς ἐνδείξεις ἐν ταῖς ἀναβάσεσι τῶν φλεγμονῶν ἀναγκαῖον ἐναντίας ὑπάρχειν. ἀθρόως γὰρ ἀπὸ τῶν στυφόντων καὶ συναγόντων τὴν οὐσίαν ἐπὶ τὰ χαλῶντα καὶ χέοντα μεταβαίνειν ἄτοπον, ἔν τε τῷ μεταξὺ τῆς ἀκμῆς τε καὶ ἀρχῆς αὐξανομένων ἔτι τῶν νοσημάτων οὔθ’ ἁπλῶς ἀποκρούεσθαι δίκαιον, ὡς ἐν ἀρχαῖς; οὔτ’ ἤδη διαφορεῖν, ὡς ἐν ἀκμαῖς, ἀλλ’ ὥσπερ ἡ κατάστασις τοῦ πάθους μέση τῶν ἐναντίων ἐστὶν, οὕτω χρὴ καὶ τὴν ἴασιν μέσην εἶναι, μήτε στύφουσαν, ὡς ἐν ταῖς ἀρχαῖς, μήτε διαφοροῦσαν, ὡς ἐν ταῖς ἀκμαῖς. καὶ δὴ ἅπαν τὸ μέσον ἐκ τῶν ἐναντίων μικτόν ἐστιν, ἤτοι δι’ ὅλων ἀλλήλοις κεραννυμένων αὐτῶν, ἢ κατὰ σμικρὰ μόρια, καὶ τὴν αἴσθησιν ἐκφεύγοντα τῶν παρακειμένων. εἴτ’ οὖν οὕτως εἴτ’ ἐκείνως βούλει κεκρᾶσθαι τὸ ῥόδινον ἐκ τῶν ἐναντίων, ἐμοὶ μὲν οὐδὲν διαφέρει, δείκνυται γὰρ ἑκατέρως τὸ προκείμενον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τὴν αἴσθησιν ἐκφεύγοντα τῶν παρακειμένων
For at the beginnings one must rather repel and check the influxes; at the peaks, disperse without biting; and in the increases, repel and disperse at the same time—a thing which someone hearing it apart from the aforesaid reasonings would perhaps 593 disbelieve. But as things now stand, I do not know whether anyone is so dull as not yet to be persuaded by the argument. For indeed the indications in the ascents of inflammations must necessarily be opposite. For to pass all at once from things that are astringent and contract the substance to those that relax and diffuse it is absurd, and in the interval between the peak and the beginning, when the diseases are still increasing, it is right neither simply to repel, as at the beginnings, nor yet to disperse, as at the peaks; but just as the condition of the affection is intermediate between the opposites, so too the cure must be intermediate, neither astringent, as at the beginnings, nor dispersing, as at the peaks. And indeed everything intermediate is mixed from the opposites, either by their being blended with one another throughout, or in small particles, the parts lying side by side also escaping perception. Whether, then, you wish rose oil to have been blended from the opposites in this way or in that, to me it makes no difference, for the point at issue is demonstrated either way.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐνεὸς, κατὰ σμικρὰ μόρια, καὶ τὴν αἴσθησιν ἐκφεύγοντα τῶν παρακειμένων, ἀδήκτως, χέοντα
For at the beginnings one must rather repel and check the influxes, at the peaks disperse without biting, and in the advances both repel and disperse at the same time; which someone hearing it without the reasonings stated before might perhaps 593 disbelieve. But now I do not know whether anyone is so dull as not yet to have been persuaded by the argument. For indeed the indications in the ascents of inflammations must necessarily be contrary. For to pass all at once from things that are astringent and that draw the substance together to things that relax and diffuse it is absurd; and in the interval between the peak and the beginning, while the diseases are still increasing, it is right neither simply to repel, as at the beginnings, nor already to disperse, as at the peaks; but just as the condition of the affection is intermediate between the contraries, so the cure too must be intermediate, neither astringent, as at the beginnings, nor dispersing, as at the peaks. And indeed every intermediate is mixed from the contraries, either with these blended with one another through and through, or in small particles of the things lying side by side, which escape perception. Whether, then, you would have rose oil blended from the contraries in this way or in that, it makes no difference to me; for the proposition is demonstrated either way.

But I know that, once you have spent more time with all the arguments about these matters, both those which Theophrastus 594 wrote and those which we set forth throughout this whole treatise, you will find the second mode of mixture. And indeed it also belongs to rose oil to penetrate into the depth and to moisten dry bodies more than olive oil does. I know, however, that if you become more fully conversant with all the reasonings on these matters, both those which Theophrastus 594 wrote and those which we set out throughout this whole treatise, you will find that it is the second mode of mixture. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κινουμένων, αὐτοῖς
For those which are simply fine-particled and hot rather disperse 595 the moistures out of the bodies which they come near to, and do not add others. Those which are at once coarse-particled and cold are by nature not able to introduce any moisture at all in the first place. But those in which there is a moisture that is at once lukewarm and fine-particled — nothing prevents these, on account of the fine-particled and at the same time lukewarm character of their substance, from being readily set in motion and passing through the pores of the bodies that are moved (κινουμένων). That they do not heat sufficiently is clear from the fact that they do not disperse the moisture present in them. And if, in addition to happening to be such, they also partake of some slight astringency, of such a kind as both to push on toward the depth whatever fine-particled part has already entered the pores, and in some way to constrict and condense the outer surface, these are the most effective of all moistening agents. For astringency, when it closes off the pores beforehand, drawing together into itself and compacting the substance it comes near to, stands as an obstacle to those things whose nature is to soak.

For whatever things are simply fine-particled and hot rather disperse 595 the moistures out of the bodies they come near, and do not add others. Those that are at once coarse-particled and cold are not naturally fitted to introduce any moisture at all in the first place. For astringency, by closing off the pores before anything else and by drawing together into itself and compacting the substance that comes near it, stands in the way of the things naturally fitted to moisten.

For if indeed it is impossible for such drugs to kill unless they also chill the heart itself, then surely they must travel inward, 597 being broken up and thinned. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὥστ’ ἔστιν. ὅτε
For it has already been shown many times that all the qualities act upon one another in some respect, and that a stone has been affected by the continuous striking of a drip of water, and a sword has been blunted while cutting wax; and it has further been demonstrated that the activities in animals come about through the solid bodies within them, so that so long as these have not yet been chilled by the cooling drugs, it is necessary 598 that the animal survive; but they are not chilled before the drugs have penetrated into their proper pores. And this does not belong of themselves to the coarse-grained and cold drugs, unless they happen upon something to guide them. What guides them, as the first cause, is the attraction of the arteries, for these were shown, in dilating, to draw everything that lies near; and, as the second and material cause, the fineness of parts of the substance, which the colder and more coarse-particled drugs, not possessing it of themselves, need others to supply. Accordingly, all bodies that are cold in their mixtures both have the attraction of the arteries weaker than hot ones—for this too was shown—and are unable quickly to break down the substances of the drugs into small parts. And in consequence the drug, delaying, is itself likely to undergo something in the course of time, so that sometimes it is even concocted; just as the juice of lettuce also is. For in time this too is concocted; but if it manages to reach the heart unimpaired, it kills much like hemlock. And all the other strongly cooling juices undergo this as well.
